Categorical centers and Yetter–Drinfel‘d-modules as 2-categorical (bi)lax structures
Bojana Femić, Sebastian Halbig

Abstract
The bicategorical point of view provides a natural setting for many concepts in the representation theory of monoidal categories. We show that centers of twisted bimodule categories correspond to categories of 2-dimensional natural transformations and modifications between the deloopings of the twisting functors. We also show that dualities lift to centers of twisted bimodule categories. Inspired by the notion of (pre)bimonoidal functors due to McCurdy and Street and by bilax functors of Aguiar and Mahajan, we study 2-dimensional functors which are simultaneously lax and colax with a compatibility condition. Our approach uses a sort of 2-categorical Yang-Baxter operators, but the idea could equally be carried out using a kind of 2-categorical braidings. We show how this concept, which we call bilax functors, generalize many known notions from the theory of Hopf algebras. We propose a 2-category of bilax functors whose 1-cells generalize the notions of Yetter-Drinfel‘d modules in ordinary categories, and a type of bimonads and mixed distributive laws in 2-categories. We show that the 2-category of bilax functors from the trivial 2-category is isomorphic to the 2-category of bimonads, and that there is a faithful 2-functor from the latter to the 2-category of mixed distributive laws of Power and Watanabe.



Keywords:
center categories, bicategories, Yang-Baxter operators, bimonads, bimonoidal functors.

2020 MSC:
18N10, 18D25, 18M15.

1
Introduction

The concept of centers of a monoids was categorified independently by Drinfel‘d, Majid and Street in the 1990’s.
Since then it has been extensively studied in Hopf algebra and category theory, see for example
[
17
]
for an overview.
One of its striking features comes from the fact that by passing from sets to categories one can replace the qualitative question: ‘Do two elements commute with another?’ with a quantitative one: ‘How many suitably coherent (iso-)morphisms
exist between the tensor product of two objects and its opposite?’.
Such (iso-)morphisms are called
half-braidings
.
The center of a monoidal category consists of objects of the underlying category with fixed half-braidings together with morphisms of the base category which satisfy a certain compatibility relation.

The aim of the present paper is twofold. In the first part, we study the center construction from the bicategorical perspective.
Our main motivation comes from the observation that monoidal categories can be identified with bicategories with a single object.
This procedure, sometimes called delooping, establishes an equivalence between monoidal categories with monoidal functors and bicategories with a single object together with pseudofunctors.
Following this line of thinking, by simple means we reveal a surprising and beautiful fact that colax natural transformations between lax
functors among bicategories with single objects are nothing but the objects of the twisted Drinfel‘d center of the corresponding codomain monoidal category. Accordingly, modifications of such colax transformations correspond to the morphisms in the Drinfel‘d center, so that one has an isomorphism of categories. In particular, we obtain:

Theorem 1.1

Let
{\mathcal{C}}
be a monoidal category and write
Del({\mathcal{C}})
for its delooping.
There exists a monoidal equivalence of categories between the Drinfel‘d center of
{\mathcal{C}}
and the category of pseudonatural
transformations and their modifications on the identitity functor of
Del({\mathcal{C}})
.

In fact, we formulate a general (weak) center category
{\mathcal{Z}}^{w}(F,{\mathcal{M}},G)
for a
{\mathcal{D}}\mbox{-}{\mathcal{C}}
-bimodule category
{\mathcal{M}}
and two lax monoidal functors

F:{\mathcal{E}}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}{\mathcal{D}}
and
G:{\mathcal{E}}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}{\mathcal{C}}
from a third monoidal category
{\mathcal{E}}
. In the case that
{\mathcal{C}}={\mathcal{D}}={\mathcal{M}}
, we interpret
{\mathcal{Z}}^{w}(F,{\mathcal{D}},G)
from a
bicategorical point of view. As this interpretation relies on delooping, in paricular on the fact that the monoidal product of
{\mathcal{C}}
becomes the composition of 1-cells, this approach does not allow for giving a bicategorical interpretation of
{\mathcal{Z}}^{w}(F,{\mathcal{M}},G)
for general
F
and
G
.
This task will be treated elsewhere. (The weakness corresponds to dealing with non-invertible half-braidings, while with strongness we allude to invertible ones. Accordingly, we
differentiate left and right weak centers. )

We prove that left weak center categories form a bicategory
{\mathcal{Z}}^{w}_{l}({\mathcal{E}},{\mathcal{K}})
which is isomorphic to the bicategory of suitable lax
functors of bicategories.
When
{\mathcal{K}}
is an autonomous 2-category, meaning that all its 1-cells have left and right adjoints, and
{\mathcal{E}}
is
an autonomous monoidal category, then on pseudofunctors
Del({\mathcal{E}})\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}{\mathcal{K}}
the weak and strong center categories coincide (Proposition
3.14
).
Furthermore, under these conditions the corresponding bicategory
{\mathcal{Z}}^{w\mbox{-}ps}_{l}({\mathcal{E}},{\mathcal{K}})
is autonomous (Proposition
3.15
).
This result provides a natural interpretation of duality notions between centers of compatible bimodule categories in
[
14
]
.
Moreover, our bicategorical interpretation of center categories
{\mathcal{Z}}^{w}_{l}(F,{\mathcal{M}},G)
which make up the bicategory
{\mathcal{Z}}^{w}_{l}({\mathcal{E}},{\mathcal{K}})

encompasses also the Shimizu’s bicategory
{\rm TF}({\mathcal{C}},{\mathcal{D}})
of tensor functors from
[
29
]
and the result thereof about duals.

The above bicategory
{\mathcal{Z}}^{w}_{l}({\mathcal{E}},{\mathcal{K}})
is a particular case of the bicategory
\operatorname{Lax}_{clx}({\mathcal{B}},{\mathcal{B}}^{\prime})
of lax functors
{\mathcal{B}}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}{\mathcal{B}}^{\prime}

among bicategories, colax natural transformations and modifications. Taking the pseudo-pseudo version of the latter,
we get the bicategory
{\rm Ps}_{ps}({\mathcal{B}},{\mathcal{B}}^{\prime})
whose hom-categories are
{\rm Ps}({\mathcal{F}},{\mathcal{G}})
for pseudofunctors
{\mathcal{F}},{\mathcal{G}}:{\mathcal{B}}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}{\mathcal{B}}^{\prime}
.
When
{\mathcal{F}}={\mathcal{G}}=\operatorname{Id}_{\mathcal{B}}
one recovers the center category
{\mathcal{Z}}({\mathcal{B}})
of the bicategory
{\mathcal{B}}
introduced in

[
25
]
. Our above bicategory of center categories alludes to the possibility to consider “twisted center categories of the bicategory
{\mathcal{B}}^{\prime}
”.

On the other hand, in the second part of the paper, we introduce and study 2-categorical functors which are simultaneously lax and colax with a compatibility relation involving a
Yang–Baxter operator.
We call them
bilax functors
and differentiate
bilax functors with compatible Yang-Baxter operator
.
For monoidal categories such functors were studied under the name of pre-bimonoidal and bimonoidal functors in
[
24
]
,
and (when the domain category is braided) bilax functors in
[
1
]
.
We show that our bilax functors generalize a variety of notions and possess certain preservation properties:
bialgebras in braided monoidal categories,
bimonads

in 2-categories (with respect to Yang-Baxter operators, YBO’s), and preserve bimonads (w.r.t. YBO’s),
bimonads in 2-categories with respect to distributive laws from
[
10
]
,
module comonads
and

comodule monads
, and
relative bimonad modules
. Moreover, the component functors of a bilax functor on hom-categories
factor through the category of
Hopf bimodules
(w.r.t. YBO’s). The 2-categorical notions in italic letters are introduced in this paper
and they generalize to 2-categories the same named notions in braided monoidal categories.

We record that instead of working with Yang-Baxter operators, one could equally use local braidings, following the footsteps of
[
1
]
.
In this case the generalization and preservation results somewhat differ from the ones that we obtained and that are listed above.

We establish a 2-category of bilax functors
{\rm Bilax}({\mathcal{K}},{\mathcal{K}}^{\prime})
by introducing bilax natural transformations and bilax modifications.
Accordingly,
{\rm Bilax}_{c}({\mathcal{K}},{\mathcal{K}}^{\prime})
denotes the 2-category of bilax functors with compatible Yang-Baxter operator.
Bilax natural transformations are both lax and colax natural transformations satisfying a compatibility condition.
As such they generalize bimonad morphisms from
[
10
]
and Yetter-Drinfel‘d modules from braided monoidal categories.
In the classical case, the category of Yetter-Drinfel‘d modules over a bialgebra
B
is monoidally equivalent to the Drinfel‘d center of
the category
{}_{B}{\mathcal{M}}
of modules over the same bialgebra. The half-braidings in the left Drinfel‘d center can be seen as colax natural transformations.
In the category
{}_{B}{\mathcal{M}}
one can construct a lax natural transformation which together with the colax one makes a bilax natural transformation.
(We explain this in more detail at the end of Subsection
5.1
.)
This illustrates why in a general 2-category
{\mathcal{K}}
bilax natural transformations (and bilax modifications) generalize the category of Yetter-Drinfel‘d modules, but not the (left) center category.
Finally, we show that there is a 2-category isomorphism
{\rm Bilax}_{c}(1,{\mathcal{K}})\cong{\rm Bimnd}({\mathcal{K}})
and a faithful 2-functor

{\rm Bimnd}({\mathcal{K}})\hookrightarrow\operatorname{Dist}({\mathcal{K}})
. Here
{\rm Bimnd}({\mathcal{K}})
is the 2-category of bimonads from
[
11
]
and
\operatorname{Dist}({\mathcal{K}})
is the 2-category
of mixed distributive laws of
[
28
]
.

The paper is composed as follows. We first give an overview of bicategories, deloopings, module and center categories. In
section 3 we give a higher categorical interpretation of center categories and study when the bicategory of center categories
is autonomous. Bilax functors and their properties are studied in section 4, while in the last section a 2-category of bilax
functors is introduced and its relations to the 2-categories
{\rm Bimnd}({\mathcal{K}})
and
\operatorname{Dist}({\mathcal{K}})
is shown.

2
Preliminaries: Deloopings and weak twisted centers

We assume that the reader is familiar with the notion of a braided monoidal category and the corresponding
notation of string diagrams (see
e.g.

[
15
,
17
,
31
]
), as well as with the definition of a bicategory,
for which we recommend
[
3
,
16
]
.

In this section we give a short summary of bicategories, delooping bicategories, module categories and weak twisted centers.
For a more extensive discussion of module categories we refer the reader to
[
7
]
.

Briefly, a
monoidal category
consists of a category
{\mathcal{C}}
together with a suitably associative and unital multiplication
\otimes\colon{\mathcal{C}}\times{\mathcal{C}}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}{\mathcal{C}}
implemented by a functor which is called the
tensor product
.

A ‘many object’ generalization of monoidal categories is provided by
bicategories
. These can be thought of as higher dimensional categories with
hom-categories
between every pair of objects instead of mere sets. The objects of these hom-categories are called

1-cells
and the morphisms
2-cells
.
Any bicategory
{\mathcal{K}}
admits two ways to compose:
horizontal composition
given by the composition functors

\circ_{Z,Y,X}\colon{\mathcal{K}}(Y,Z)\times{\mathcal{K}}(X,Y)\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}{\mathcal{K}}(X,Z),\hskip 18.49988pt\hskip 18.49988pt\text{ for }X,Y,Z\in{\mathcal{O}b}{\mathcal{K}}\text{ (objects of ${\mathcal{K}}$)},

and
vertical composition
induced by the compositions inside the hom-categories.
Instead of identity morphisms, every
X\in{\mathcal{O}b}{\mathcal{K}}
has a unit 1-cell
\operatorname{id}_{X}\in{\mathcal{K}}(X,X)
.
In general, the horizontal composition of a bicategory is associative and unital only up to suitable natural isomorphisms.
Bicategories where these morphisms are identities are called
2-categories
.
Since every bicategory is biequivalent to a 2-category, we will restrict ourselves without loss of generality
to the setting of 2-categories.

As hinted at before, there is an intimate relationship between monoidal categories and bicategories. It is provided by considering a monoidal category
{\mathcal{C}}
as a bicategory
Del({\mathcal{C}})
with one object (which we will usually denote by
*
) and
{\mathcal{C}}
as its unique hom-category. Under this identification, the tensor product of
{\mathcal{C}}
becomes the horizontal composition of
Del({\mathcal{C}})
, and the monoidal unit
becomes the identity 1-cell
id
on the unique object of
Del({\mathcal{C}})
.
The resulting canoncial isomorphism of categories between the category of monoidal categories with certain structure preserving functors and one-object bicategories plus structure preserving 2-dimensional functors is called
delooping
:

Del\colon\left\{\begin{gathered}\text{monoidal categories with}\\
\text{lax/colax/strong monoidal functors}\end{gathered}\right\}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\left\{\begin{gathered}\text{one-object bicategories with}\\
\text{lax/colax/pseudofunctors}.\end{gathered}\right\}

(1)

Remark 2.1

Observe that we consider the horizontal composition in bicategories in the counter lexicographical order, whereas
the tensor product in a monoidal category
{\mathcal{C}}
is read from left to right, that is:
{\mathcal{C}}\times{\mathcal{C}}\ni(X,Y)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\otimes}}{{\mapsto}}X\otimes Y\in{\mathcal{C}}
.
For objects
X,Y\in{\mathcal{C}}
corresponding to 1-cells
x,y
in
Del({\mathcal{C}})
respectively, this implies that the tensor product
X\otimes Y
corresponds to the composition of 1-cells
y\circ x
. In order to avoid applying this mirror symmetry, we are going to consider in the isomorphism
Del

in (
1
) that the reversed tensor product becomes the horizontal composition in bicategories (formally, this is precomposing

Del
with the isomorphism functor defined on objects by sending a monoidal category
({\mathcal{C}},\otimes)
to it reversed category
({\mathcal{C}}^{rev},\otimes^{rev})
).

Bicategories provide a natural interpretation of the representation theory of monoidal categories.
All endomorphism categories of a bicategory are monoidal with horizontal composition as a tensor product.
Similarly, given two objects
A,B\in{\mathcal{O}b}{\mathcal{K}}
of a bicategory with endomorphism categories
{\mathcal{D}}:={\mathcal{K}}(A,A)
and
{\mathcal{C}}:={\mathcal{K}}(B,B)
,
horizontal composition endows
{\mathcal{M}}\coloneqq{\mathcal{K}}(A,B)
with the structure of a
({\mathcal{C}},{\mathcal{D}})
-
bimodule category
.
That is, there are two functors
\rhd\colon{\mathcal{C}}\times{\mathcal{M}}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}{\mathcal{M}}
and
\lhd\colon{\mathcal{M}}\times{\mathcal{D}}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}{\mathcal{D}}
subject to analogous but weakened version of the axioms of bimodules over a monoid.

Conversely: to any
({\mathcal{C}},{\mathcal{D}})
-bimodule category
{\mathcal{M}}
we can associate a two object bicategory
Del({\mathcal{M}})
, which we call the

delooping
of
{\mathcal{M}}
. It has two objects
0
and
1
and hom-categories
Del({\mathcal{M}})(0,0)={\mathcal{D}},Del({\mathcal{M}})(0,1)=M,Del({\mathcal{M}})(1,1)={\mathcal{C}}
and

Del({\mathcal{M}})(1,0)=1
, the trivial category. Horizontal composition is given by
the tensor products of
{\mathcal{C}}
and
{\mathcal{D}}
and the left and right action of
{\mathcal{C}}
and
{\mathcal{D}}
on
{\mathcal{M}}
. The relation between (bi)module categories and bicategories
was already observed by Benaboú,
[
3
, Section 2.3]
.

If the categories
{\mathcal{C}}
and
{\mathcal{D}}
coincide, one can define the center of a bimodule category.
The aim of the paper at hand will be the study of these centers and their interaction with the theory of bicategories in a slightly more general version.

Definition 2.2

Let
F\colon{\mathcal{E}}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}{\mathcal{D}}
and
G\colon{\mathcal{E}}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}{\mathcal{C}}
be lax monoidal functors and
{\mathcal{M}}
a (strict)
({\mathcal{C}},{\mathcal{D}})
-bimodule category over the (strict) monoidal categories
{\mathcal{C}}
and
{\mathcal{D}}
.
A
left half-braiding
of an object
M\in{\mathcal{M}}
relative to
F
and
G
is a natural transformation

\sigma_{X}\colon M\lhd F(X)\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}G(X)\rhd M,\hskip 18.49988pt\hskip 18.49988pt\text{for all }X\in{\mathcal{E}},

such that for all
X,Y\in\mathcal{E}
the following diagrams commute:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{M\lhd F(Y)\lhd F(X)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G(Y)\rhd M\lhd F(X)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{M\lhd F(Y\otimes X)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G(Y\otimes X)\rhd M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G(Y)\rhd G(X)\rhd M}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma_{Y}\lhd\operatorname{id}_{F(X)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{id}_{G(Y)}\lhd\sigma_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G^{2}\rhd M}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M\lhd F^{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma_{Y\otimes X}}

(2)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{M\lhd I\cong I\rhd M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G(I)\rhd M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{M\lhd F(I)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G^{0}\rhd M}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M\lhd F^{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma_{I}}

(3)

Similarly, a
right half-braiding
on
M
relative to
F
and
G
is a natural transformation

\tilde{\sigma}_{X}\colon G(X)\rhd M\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}M\lhd F(X),\hskip 18.49988pt\hskip 18.49988pt\text{for all }X\in{\mathcal{E}},

subject to analogous identities.

The
left weak center of
{\mathcal{M}}
relative to
F
and
G
is the category
{\mathcal{Z}}_{l}^{w}(F,{\mathcal{M}},G)
.
Its objects are pairs
(M,\sigma)
consisting of an object
M\in{\mathcal{M}}
together with a left half-braiding
\sigma
on
M
relative to
F
and
G
.
A morphism between objects
(M,\sigma),(N,\tau)\in{\mathcal{Z}}_{l}^{w}(F,{\mathcal{M}},G)
is an arrow
f\in{\mathcal{M}}(M,N)
such that

(\operatorname{id}_{G(X)}\rhd f)\sigma_{X}=\tau_{X}(f\lhd\operatorname{id}_{F(X)}),\hskip 18.49988pt\hskip 18.49988pt\text{for all }X\in{\mathcal{E}}.

(4)

The full subcategory
{\mathcal{Z}}^{s}_{l}(F,{\mathcal{M}},G)
of
{\mathcal{Z}}_{l}^{w}(F,{\mathcal{M}},G)
whose objects have invertible half-braidings is called the
(strong) left center of
{\mathcal{M}}
relative to
F
and
G
. When the functors are clear from the context, we will call the latter two categories simply

left weak/strong twisted centers of
{\mathcal{M}}
, respectively.

We define the right weak and strong twisted center categories
{\mathcal{Z}}_{r}^{w}(F,{\mathcal{M}},G)
and
{\mathcal{Z}}^{s}_{r}(F,{\mathcal{M}},G)
in an analogous way.

When
{\mathcal{C}}={\mathcal{D}}={\mathcal{M}}
we set
Z_{l}^{w}(F,G):={\mathcal{Z}}_{l}^{w}(F,{\mathcal{D}},G)
and
Z_{r}^{w}(F,G):={\mathcal{Z}}_{r}^{w}(F,{\mathcal{D}},G)
.
For
{\mathcal{C}}
a tensor category and
F,G
tensor functors these present the (left and right version of) twisted center category
Z(F,G)

studied in
[
29
, Section 3]
.

In case
F=G=\operatorname{Id}_{\mathcal{C}}
, we write
{\mathcal{Z}}^{l}_{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{M}})\coloneqq{\mathcal{Z}}^{s}_{l}(\operatorname{Id},{\mathcal{M}},\operatorname{Id})
and
{\mathcal{Z}}^{r}_{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{M}})\coloneqq{\mathcal{Z}}^{s}_{r}(\operatorname{Id},{\mathcal{M}},\operatorname{Id})
.
These recover the (left and right) center category from
[
12
]
. If moreover
{\mathcal{M}}={\mathcal{C}}
, the categories
{\mathcal{Z}}^{l}_{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{C}})
and
{\mathcal{Z}}^{r}_{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{C}})

recover the left and right Drinfel‘d center categories of
{\mathcal{C}}
.

Lemma 2.3

Suppose
G\colon{\mathcal{E}}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}{\mathcal{C}}
and
F\colon{\mathcal{E}}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}{\mathcal{D}}
are lax monoidal functors and
{\mathcal{M}}
is a
({\mathcal{C}},{\mathcal{D}})
-bimodule category.
Then there exists an isomorphism of categories

\Xi\colon{\mathcal{Z}}_{l}^{s}(F,{\mathcal{M}},G)\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}{\mathcal{Z}}_{r}^{s}(F,{\mathcal{M}},G),\hskip 18.49988pt\hskip 18.49988pt\Xi(M,\sigma)=(M,\sigma^{-1}),

(5)

which is the identity on morphisms.

Proof.

Suppose that
\sigma
is an invertible left half-braiding on an object
M\in{\mathcal{M}}
.
We show that
\sigma^{-1}
defines a right half-braiding. Precomposing the equation in (
2
) by

(\sigma_{Y}^{-1}\lhd\operatorname{id}_{F(X)})(\operatorname{id}_{G(X)}\rhd\sigma_{X}^{-1})
and postcomposing by
\sigma_{YX}^{-1}
yields
the desired compatibility of right half-braidings with the lax functor structures.
Analogous calculations show that
\sigma^{-1}
is compatible with the lax units of
F
and
G
and that
\Xi
sends any morphism
in the strong left center to a morphism of the strong right center.
The proof is concluded by constructing
\Xi^{-1}
in the same spirit as
\Xi
.
That is, by mapping invertible right half-braidings to their inverses.
∎∎

The construction of (left) strong twisted center categories can be seen as a result of the following composition of 2-functors:

{\mathcal{C}}\mbox{-}{\mathcal{D}}\mbox{-}\operatorname{Bimod}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(F,G)}}{{\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}}}{\mathcal{E}}\mbox{-}{\mathcal{E}}\mbox{-}\operatorname{Bimod}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\mathcal{Z}}_{\mathcal{E}}}}{{\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}}}{\mathcal{Z}}({\mathcal{E}})\mbox{-}\operatorname{Mod}

\hskip 28.45274pt{\mathcal{M}}\hskip 22.76228pt\mapsto\hskip 17.07182pt{}_{G}{\mathcal{M}}_{F}\hskip 22.76228pt\mapsto\hskip 11.38092pt{\mathcal{Z}}_{\mathcal{E}}({}_{G}{\mathcal{M}}_{F})

where
(F,G)
denotes precomposing the left and right action by
F
and
G
, respectively, and
{\mathcal{Z}}_{\mathcal{E}}
is defined as in
[
8
, Section 3.4]
. The term “twisted” is motivated by this composition. Namely, if
F
and
G

are strong monoidal functors, a
{\mathcal{C}}\mbox{-}{\mathcal{D}}
-bimodule category structure is twisted by them into an
{\mathcal{E}}
-bimodule structure.

3
Categorical centers as a data in a tricategory

At the core of our investigation in this section are (weak) twisted centers and their interpretation from a higher categorical point of view.
We will show that center categories are hom-categories of hom-bicategories of a particular tricategory.
Namely, the tricategory of bicategories with a single object.

3.1
Categorical centers as (co)lax natural transformations

For the interpretation of center categories from the perspective of 2-categories we first recall the definitions of lax
and colax functors between bicategories and of lax and colax natural transformations between the latter.

Definition 3.1

A
lax functor

({\mathcal{F}},{\mathcal{F}}^{2},{\mathcal{F}}^{0})\colon{\mathcal{K}}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}{\mathcal{K}}^{\prime}
between 2-categories consists of

1.

an assignment
{\mathcal{O}b}{\mathcal{K}}\ni A\mapsto{\mathcal{F}}(A)\in{\mathcal{O}b}{\mathcal{K}}^{\prime}
,

2.

for all
A,B\in{\mathcal{O}b}{\mathcal{K}}
a local functor
{\mathcal{F}}_{A,B}\colon{\mathcal{K}}(A,B)\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}{\mathcal{K}}^{\prime}({\mathcal{F}}(A),{\mathcal{F}}(B))
,

3.

a natural transformation

{\mathcal{F}}^{2}_{g,f}\colon F(g)\otimes^{\prime}F(f)\Rightarrow F(g\otimes f),\hskip 18.49988pt\hskip 18.49988pt\text{ for }(g,f)\in{\mathcal{K}}(B,C)\times{\mathcal{K}}(A,B),

and

4.

a natural transformation

{\mathcal{F}}^{0}_{A}\colon id_{{\mathcal{F}}(A)}\Rightarrow{\mathcal{F}}(id_{A}),\hskip 18.49988pt\hskip 18.49988pt\text{ for }A\in{\mathcal{O}b}{\mathcal{K}},

so that
{\mathcal{F}}^{2}
and
{\mathcal{F}}^{0}
satisfy
associativity
and
unitality
laws.

When the natural transformations
{\mathcal{F}}^{2}
and
{\mathcal{F}}^{0}
are directed in the opposite direction
and satisfy
coassociativity
and
counitality
laws, one has a
colax functor
.
One speaks about a
pseudofunctor
if
{\mathcal{F}}^{2}
and
{\mathcal{F}}^{0}
are isomorphisms.

Lax transformations can be defined both for lax and colax functors. The same holds for colax transformations, so that there are four
variations of definitions, depending on the situation.

Definition 3.2

Let
({\mathcal{F}},{\mathcal{F}}^{2},{\mathcal{F}}^{0})\colon{\mathcal{K}}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}{\mathcal{K}}^{\prime}
and
({\mathcal{G}},{\mathcal{G}}^{2},{\mathcal{G}}^{0})\colon{\mathcal{K}}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}{\mathcal{K}}^{\prime}
be lax functors between 2-categories.
A colax natural transformation
\chi\colon{\mathcal{F}}\Rightarrow{\mathcal{G}}
consists of

1.

a 1-cell
\chi_{A}\colon{\mathcal{F}}(A)\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}{\mathcal{G}}(A)
for each object
A\in{\mathcal{O}b}{\mathcal{K}}
, and

2.

for every pair of objects
A,B\in Ob{\mathcal{K}}
a collection of 2-cells

\{\chi_{f}\colon\chi_{B}\circ{\mathcal{F}}_{A,B}(f)\Rightarrow{\mathcal{G}}_{A,B}(f)\circ\chi_{A}\mid f\in{\mathcal{K}}(A,B)\}

(6)

natural in
f
subject to
colax multiplicativity

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathcal{F}}(B)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathcal{F}}(A)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathcal{F}}(B)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathcal{F}}(C)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathcal{F}}(A)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathcal{F}}(C)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{=}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathcal{G}}(A)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathcal{G}}(B)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathcal{G}}(C)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathcal{G}}(A)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathcal{G}}(C)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{F}}(f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{F}}(g)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chi_{C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{G}}(g)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chi_{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chi_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{G}}(f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{G}}(gf)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{F}}(f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{F}}(g)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{F}}(gf)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{G}}(gf)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chi_{C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chi_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chi_{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chi_{g}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chi_{gf}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{F}}^{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{G}}^{2}}

(7)

and
colax unitality

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathcal{F}}(A)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathcal{F}}(A)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathcal{F}}(A)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathcal{F}}(A)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{=}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathcal{G}}(A)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathcal{G}}(A)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathcal{G}}(A)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathcal{G}}(A)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chi_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chi_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{id}_{{\mathcal{G}}(A)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{G}}(\operatorname{id}_{A})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{id}_{{\mathcal{F}}(A)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{id}_{{\mathcal{F}}(A)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{F}}(\operatorname{id}_{A})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chi_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chi_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{G}}(\operatorname{id}_{A})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chi_{\operatorname{id}_{A}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{F}}^{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{G}}^{0}}

(8)

If the 2-cells of
\chi
are invertible, it is called a
pseudonatural transformation
. In case they are identities, one speaks of a
strict natural transformation
.

By reverting the direction of the 2-cells of
\chi
one obtains the notion of a
lax natural transformation
between lax functors.

We start by a simple observation that entails a marvelous fact.

Proposition 3.3

Let
F,G\colon{\mathcal{C}}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}{\mathcal{D}}
be lax monoidal functors. The objects of the weak twisted center
{\mathcal{Z}}_{l}^{w}(F,{\mathcal{D}},G)
are canonically in bijection with colax natural transformations
\chi\colon Del(F)\Rightarrow Del(G)
between the induced lax functors
Del(F),Del(G):Del({\mathcal{C}})\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}Del({\mathcal{D}})
.
Under this identification, the objects of the strong center correspond to pseudonatural transformations.

Proof.

Since both bicategories
Del({\mathcal{C}}),Del({\mathcal{D}})
have a single object, there is a single 1-cell component of
\chi\colon Del(F)\Rightarrow Del(G)
, which is a distinguished object
D_{\chi}=\chi_{*}
in
{\mathcal{D}}
. The 2-cell components of
\chi
amount to
morphisms
\chi_{X}:D_{\chi}\otimes F(X)\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}G(X)\otimes D_{\chi}
in
{\mathcal{D}}
natural in
X
(mind that we do not flip the order of factors when translating from
Del({\mathcal{C}})
to
{\mathcal{D}}
, as we assume the reversed tensor product in the sense of Remark
2.1
), and the colax multiplicativity and unity translate into the commuting diagrams (
2
) and (
3
). The second claim is immediate.
∎∎

Remark 3.4

An analogous statement to the previous proposition for right half-braidings can be obtained by considering lax instead of colax natural transformations between lax functors.

To obtain a bicategorical interpretation of the morphisms in a center category, we need to recall the definition of modifications.

Definition 3.5

A modification
a:\chi\Rrightarrow\psi
between two colax natural transformations
\chi,\psi:{\mathcal{F}}\Rightarrow{\mathcal{G}}:{\mathcal{K}}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}{\mathcal{K}}^{\prime}
consists of a family of 2-cells
a_{A}:\chi(A)\Rightarrow\psi(A)
, indexed by the objects
A\in{\mathcal{O}b}{\mathcal{K}}
, such that for every 1-cell
f\in{\mathcal{K}}(A,B)
we have:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathcal{F}}(A)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathcal{G}}(A)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathcal{F}}(A)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathcal{G}}(A)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{=}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathcal{F}}(B)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathcal{G}}(B)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathcal{F}}(B)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathcal{G}}(B)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chi_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G(f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chi_{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chi_{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G(f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi_{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chi_{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi_{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{B}}

(9)

For two lax functors
{\mathcal{F}},{\mathcal{G}}:{\mathcal{B}}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}{\mathcal{B}}^{\prime}
among bicategories let
\text{Colax}({\mathcal{F}},{\mathcal{G}})
and
\text{Lax}({\mathcal{F}},{\mathcal{G}})
denote the
categories of colax (respectively lax) natural transformations and their modifications.
Similarly,
\text{Pseudo}(F,G)
denotes the category of pseudonatural transformations and their modifications.

Proposition 3.6

Let
F,G\colon{\mathcal{C}}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}{\mathcal{D}}
be lax monoidal functors. There are canonical isomorphisms of categories:

{\mathcal{Z}}_{l}^{w}(F,{\mathcal{D}},G)\cong\text{Colax}(Del(F),Del(G)),\hskip 18.49988pt\hskip 18.49988pt{\mathcal{Z}}_{l}^{s}(F,{\mathcal{D}},G)\cong\text{Pseudo}(Del(F),Del(G)),

{\mathcal{Z}}_{r}^{w}(F,{\mathcal{D}},G)\cong\text{Lax}(Del(F),Del(G)),\hskip 18.49988pt\hskip 18.49988pt{\mathcal{Z}}_{r}^{s}(F,{\mathcal{D}},G)\cong\text{Pseudo}(Del(F),Del(G)).

Proof.

We only prove the claims for left center categories, as the other cases are analogous.
Let
\chi,\psi\colon Del(F)\Rightarrow Del(G)
be colax natural transformations and write
(D_{\chi},\chi),(E_{\psi},\psi)\in{\mathcal{Z}}_{w}^{l}(F,{\mathcal{D}},G)
for their corresponding objects in the weak left center.
Since
Del({\mathcal{C}})
and
Del({\mathcal{C}})
are deloopings of monoidal categories, any modification
a\colon\chi\Rrightarrow\psi
is defined by a single
morphism
f\colon D_{\chi}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}E_{\psi}
satisfying for all
X\in{\mathcal{C}}
the following identity:

\begin{minipage}[24.0pt]\begin{picture}\put(0.0,60.0){\line{1}{0}{0.0}}\put(6.0,64.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0)[b]{$\scriptstyle (10)

This is precisely the defining equation of a morphism in the weak center and the claim follows.
∎∎

In the above proposition we started from two lax monoidal functors between monoidal categories to obtain the result. One can also start
from two lax functors between 2-categories in a specific way to obtain an analogous result. For this purpose recall the interplay
between ordinary categories and bicategories encoded in the delooping isomorphism (
1
) and the discussion after Remark
2.1
.

Proposition 3.7

Let
{\mathcal{E}}
be a monoidal category,
{\mathcal{K}}
a bicategory which has at least two objects
0
and
1
, and assume that
{\mathcal{F}},{\mathcal{G}}:Del({\mathcal{E}})\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}{\mathcal{K}}

are two lax functors such that
{\mathcal{F}}(*)=0
and
{\mathcal{G}}(*)=1
. There are canonical isomorphisms of categories

{\mathcal{Z}}_{l}^{w}(F,{\mathcal{M}},G)\cong\text{Colax}({\mathcal{F}},{\mathcal{G}}),\hskip 18.49988pt\hskip 18.49988pt{\mathcal{Z}}_{l}^{s}(F,{\mathcal{M}},G)\cong\text{Pseudo}({\mathcal{F}},{\mathcal{G}}),

{\mathcal{Z}}_{r}^{w}(F,{\mathcal{M}},G)\cong\text{Lax}({\mathcal{F}},{\mathcal{G}}),\hskip 18.49988pt\hskip 18.49988pt{\mathcal{Z}}_{r}^{s}(F,{\mathcal{M}},G)\cong\text{Pseudo}({\mathcal{F}},{\mathcal{G}}),

for a suitable bimodule category
{\mathcal{M}}
and lax monoidal functors
F
and
G
.

Proof.

We set
{\mathcal{D}}:={\mathcal{K}}(0,0),{\mathcal{C}}:={\mathcal{K}}(1,1)
and
{\mathcal{M}}:={\mathcal{K}}(0,1)
. Then the two lax functors yield lax monoidal functors
F={\mathcal{F}}_{*,*}:{\mathcal{E}}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}{\mathcal{D}},G={\mathcal{G}}_{*,*}:{\mathcal{E}}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}{\mathcal{C}}
.
The unique 1-cell component of a colax natural transformation
\chi:{\mathcal{F}}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}{\mathcal{G}}
is an object
M
living in the
({\mathcal{C}}\mbox{-}{\mathcal{D}})
-bimodule category
{\mathcal{M}}
,
as it is a 1-cell mapping
0={\mathcal{F}}(*)\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}{\mathcal{G}}(*)=1
in
{\mathcal{K}}
. The 2-cell component yields a half-braiding given by morphisms

\chi_{X}:M\lhd F(X)\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}G(X)\rhd M
in
{\mathcal{M}}
, for every 1-endocell in
{\mathcal{K}}
,
i.e.
an object
X\in{\mathcal{E}}
. The rest follows
as in Proposition
3.6
.
∎∎

Recall that we recovered the left center category from
[
12
]
as
{\mathcal{Z}}^{l}_{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{M}})\coloneqq{\mathcal{Z}}^{s}_{l}(\operatorname{Id},{\mathcal{M}},\operatorname{Id})
, and similarly the right one,
and that they are isomorphic by Lemma
2.3
.

Corollary 3.8

Let
{\mathcal{C}}
be a monoidal category,
{\mathcal{K}}
a bicategory which has at least two objects
0
and
1
, and assume that
{\mathcal{F}},{\mathcal{G}}:Del({\mathcal{C}})\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}{\mathcal{K}}

are two pseudofunctors such that
{\mathcal{F}}(*)=0,{\mathcal{G}}(*)=1
and
{\mathcal{F}}_{*,*}={\mathcal{G}}_{*,*}=\operatorname{Id}_{\mathcal{C}}
. There are canonical isomorphisms of categories

{\mathcal{Z}}^{l}_{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{M}})\cong{\rm Pseudo}({\mathcal{F}},{\mathcal{G}})\cong{\mathcal{Z}}^{r}_{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{M}})

where
{\mathcal{M}}={\mathcal{K}}(0,1)
.

3.2
The bicategory of center categories

The bicategorical perspective gives us a deeper insight of “why” the twisted center categories can be composed between each other,
and in particular “why” “
F\mbox{-}F
-twisted” center categories are monoidal. Namely, for fixed bicategories
{\mathcal{B}}
and
{\mathcal{B}}^{\prime}
there are bicategories

\operatorname{Lax}_{lx}({\mathcal{B}},{\mathcal{B}}^{\prime})
and
\operatorname{Lax}_{clx}({\mathcal{B}},{\mathcal{B}}^{\prime})
of lax functors
{\mathcal{B}}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}{\mathcal{B}}^{\prime}
, lax (resp. colax) transformations and their modifications, see
[
16
, Theorem 4.4.11]
.
The composition of (co)lax natural transformations (as 1-cells in these bicategories) is given by what is known as the vertical composition of such transformations. The hom-categories are
\text{Lax}({\mathcal{F}},{\mathcal{G}})
and
\text{Colax}({\mathcal{F}},{\mathcal{G}})
for two lax functors
{\mathcal{F}},{\mathcal{G}}
, respectively. The horizontal composition
in the two bicategories corresponds to the composition of the right, respectively left, weak twisted center categories between themselves.
In particular, the fact that
\operatorname{Lax}({\mathcal{F}},{\mathcal{F}})
and
\text{Colax}({\mathcal{F}},{\mathcal{F}})
are monoidal entails that the “
F\mbox{-}F
-twisted”
center categories are monoidal as well. We explain now how this is achieved.

Let us fix a monoidal category
{\mathcal{E}}
and a 2-category
{\mathcal{K}}
of which we think as a collection of monoidal categories and composable bimodule categories between them. According to Proposition
3.7
, we can identify the hom-categories of
\operatorname{Lax}_{clx}(Del({\mathcal{E}}),{\mathcal{K}})
with the weak left centers relative to lax monoidal functors from
{\mathcal{E}}
to the endo-1-categories of
{\mathcal{K}}
.
In order to emphasize this point of view, we write
{\mathcal{Z}}_{l}^{w}({\mathcal{E}},{\mathcal{K}})\coloneqq\operatorname{Lax}_{clx}(Del({\mathcal{E}}),{\mathcal{K}})
.
Given lax functors
{\mathcal{F}},{\mathcal{G}}\colon Del({\mathcal{E}})\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}{\mathcal{K}}
, we write
{\mathcal{Z}}_{l}^{w}({\mathcal{F}},{\mathcal{K}},{\mathcal{G}})
for the hom-category of
{\mathcal{Z}}_{l}^{w}({\mathcal{E}},{\mathcal{K}})
with source
{\mathcal{F}}

and target
{\mathcal{G}}
. (Observe that they are categories
{\mathcal{Z}}_{l}^{w}(F,{\mathcal{M}},G)
from Proposition
3.7
, where
F,G
are lax monoidal functors
induced by
{\mathcal{F}},{\mathcal{G}}
and
{\mathcal{M}}={\mathcal{K}}(0,1)
.)

The above observation readily implies that (weak left) centers can be organized into a bicategory. We refer to
{\mathcal{Z}}_{l}^{w}({\mathcal{E}},{\mathcal{K}})
as the

bicategory of weak left centers of
{\mathcal{K}}
relative to
{\mathcal{E}}
. Our next result translates its horizontal composition into a
‘pre-tensor product’ between weak relative centers. Given two vertically composable 2-cells
x\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\alpha}}{{\Rightarrow}}y\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\beta}}{{\Rightarrow}}z:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B
, we denote their vertical composition in equations by the fraction
\frac{\alpha}{\beta}
.

Proposition 3.9

Let
{\mathcal{E}}
be a monoidal category and
{\mathcal{K}}
a 2-category. The weak left centers of
{\mathcal{K}}
relative to
{\mathcal{E}}
form a bicategory

{\mathcal{Z}}_{l}^{w}({\mathcal{E}},{\mathcal{K}})
. For all lax functors
{\mathcal{F}},{\mathcal{G}},{\mathcal{H}}:Del({\mathcal{E}})\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}{\mathcal{K}}
,
its horizontal composition is given by

\displaystyle\circ_{{\mathcal{F}},{\mathcal{G}},{\mathcal{H}}}\colon{\mathcal{Z}}_{l}^{w}({\mathcal{G}},{\mathcal{K}},{\mathcal{H}})\times{\mathcal{Z}}_{l}^{w}({\mathcal{F}},{\mathcal{K}},{\mathcal{G}})

\displaystyle\allowbreak\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}{\mathcal{Z}}_{l}^{w}({\mathcal{F}},{\mathcal{K}},{\mathcal{H}})

(11)

\displaystyle\left((N,\tau),(M,\sigma)\right)

\displaystyle\mapsto(N\circ M,\frac{\operatorname{id}_{N}\circ\sigma}{\tau\circ\operatorname{id}_{M}})

\displaystyle(g,f)

\displaystyle\mapsto(g\circ f)

Proof.

The first claim is merely a recapitulation that
{\mathcal{Z}}_{l}^{w}({\mathcal{E}},{\mathcal{K}})=\operatorname{Lax}_{clx}(Del({\mathcal{E}}),{\mathcal{K}})
is a bicategory.
By Proposition
3.7
, the objects and morphisms of any weak center are in correspondence with appropriate colax natural
transformations and modifications.
Applying this identification, one immediately obtains the above formulas from their respective composition rules.
(Observe that
M\in{}_{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathcal{D}}={\mathcal{K}}(0,1)
and
N\in{}_{\mathcal{P}}{\mathcal{N}}_{\mathcal{C}}={\mathcal{K}}(1,2)
, where
{\mathcal{F}}(*)=0,{\mathcal{G}}(*)=1
and
{\mathcal{H}}(*)=2
, and

{\mathcal{D}}:={\mathcal{K}}(0,0),{\mathcal{C}}:={\mathcal{K}}(1,1)
and
{\mathcal{P}}:={\mathcal{K}}(2,2)
, in the sense of the proof of Proposition
3.7
.)
∎∎

Analogous considerations hold for the bicategory of weak right centers
{\mathcal{Z}}_{r}^{w}({\mathcal{E}},{\mathcal{K}})\coloneqq\operatorname{Lax}_{lx}(Del({\mathcal{E}}),{\mathcal{K}})
.

The consequences of the above result are best exemplified by considering the bicategory
{\mathcal{Z}}_{l}^{w}({\mathcal{E}},{\mathcal{E}})
of all twisted weak left centers

{\mathcal{Z}}_{l}^{w}(F,{\mathcal{E}},G)
of
{\mathcal{E}}
. The weak left analogue
{\mathcal{Z}}_{l}^{w}({\mathcal{E}})
of the Drinfel‘d center corresponds to the endo-category on the identity functor of
{\mathcal{E}}
.
The previous result recovers its monoidal structure.
Moreover, it implies that, if only the left or right action of
{\mathcal{E}}
on itself is twisted, the resulting center canonically becomes a right, respectively, left module category over
{\mathcal{Z}}_{l}^{w}({\mathcal{E}})
.
This gives a theoretical justification to the constructions of
[
14
]
involving the anti-Drinfel‘d center and its opposite.

Let us consider the pseudo-pseudo version of the bicategories
\operatorname{Lax}_{lx}({\mathcal{B}},{\mathcal{B}}^{\prime})
and
\operatorname{Lax}_{clx}({\mathcal{B}},{\mathcal{B}}^{\prime})
. It is a
bicategory that we denote by
{\rm Ps}_{ps}({\mathcal{B}},{\mathcal{B}}^{\prime})
with hom-categories
{\rm Ps}({\mathcal{F}},{\mathcal{G}})
for pseudofunctors
{\mathcal{F}},{\mathcal{G}}:{\mathcal{B}}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}{\mathcal{B}}^{\prime}
.
In the particular case when
{\mathcal{F}}={\mathcal{G}}=\operatorname{Id}_{\mathcal{B}}
we have the center category
{\mathcal{Z}}({\mathcal{B}})
of the bicategory
{\mathcal{B}}
introduced in

[
25
]
. Our above considerations allude to the possibility to consider “twisted center categories of the bicategory
{\mathcal{B}}^{\prime}
”.
In this case the twisting is done through the pseudo- (or lax or colax) functors
{\mathcal{B}}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}{\mathcal{B}}^{\prime}
.

3.3
The tricategory that encomapsses strong center categories

Let
{\rm Bicat}
denote the tricategory of bicategories, pseudofunctors, pseudonatural transformations, and modifications.

Remark 3.10

Although it is sufficient to consider only lax functors and colax (resp. lax) natural transformations in order to recover left
(resp. right) weak twisted center category, in order to form a tricategory whose 2- and 3-cells are some kind of bicategorical functors
and transformations,
both kinds of cells should be of
pseudo
type,
i.e.
they both should have isomorphisms for their respective defining structures.
Namely, on one hand, in order to be able to define the horizontal composition of two lax (or colax) natural transformations, both lax and
colax structures of the functors they act to are needed (see
e.g.
Second problem in
[
18
]
).
On the other hand, one sees from the diagram (11.3.12) of
[
16
, Chapter 11.3]
that in order to construct the isomorphism
interchange 3-cell one needs both pseudofunctors and pseudonatural transformations.

One clearly has:

Proposition 3.11

By delooping, the full sub-tricategory of
{\rm Bicat}
whose objects are bicategories with a single object gives rise to a tricategory

{\rm Bicat}^{*}
with monoidal categories as objects, strong monoidal functors as 1-cells, and for each pair
F,G\colon{\mathcal{C}}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}{\mathcal{D}}

of such functors the strong center
{\mathcal{Z}}_{l}^{s}(F,{\mathcal{D}},G)\cong{\mathcal{Z}}_{r}^{s}(F,{\mathcal{D}},G)
as hom-category.

In view of Remark
3.10
it becomes clear why we do not speak of a tricategory that contains weak center categories as
bottom hom-categories.

By further restricting the tricategory
{\rm Bicat}^{*}
to finite tensor categories (in the sense of
[
7
]
), one has that every hom-bicategory

{\rm Bicat}^{*}({\mathcal{C}},{\mathcal{D}})
is precisely the bicategory
{\rm TF}({\mathcal{C}},{\mathcal{D}})
from
[
29
, Section 3.2]
, of tensor functors between finite tensor categories
{\mathcal{C}}
and
{\mathcal{D}}
.
As the author works in a context of rigid categories, he shows that for every hom-category
Z_{l}^{w}(F,G)
of the bicategory

{\rm TF}({\mathcal{C}},{\mathcal{D}})
and every object
(V,\sigma_{V})\in Z_{l}^{w}(F,G)
the transformation
\sigma_{V}
is invertible (
[
29
, Lemma 3.1]
), and

(V,\sigma_{V})
has a left and a right dual object in
Z_{l}^{w}(G,F)
. We will generalize this to 2-categories in Proposition
3.14
.

Question.
In Proposition
3.7
we dealt with lax functors
{\mathcal{F}},{\mathcal{G}}:Del({\mathcal{E}})\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}{\mathcal{K}}^{\prime}
from a one-object bicategory.
If we allow for the domain bicategory

{\mathcal{K}}
to have more than one object, we wonder what the categories of (co)lax transformations
\chi:{\mathcal{F}}\Rightarrow{\mathcal{G}}
recover. In particular,
what is the “meaning in nature” of the 2-cell components
\chi_{X}:\chi(B)\circ F(X)\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}G(X)\circ\chi(A)
for 1-cells
X:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B
in
{\mathcal{K}}
?

3.4
String diagrams in 2-categories

In Subsection
3.5
and throughout Section
4
and Section
5
we will use string diagrams for 2-categories
(again relying on the biequivalence of any bicategory with a 2-category).
Our string diagrams are read from top to bottom and (in the context of 2-categories) from right to left. The domains and codomains
of the strings stand for 1-cells, while the strings themselves and boxes stand for 2-cells. The 0-cells are to be understood from the context
(reading the 1-cells from right to left). Observe that such string diagrams which depict 2-cells in a 2-category
{\mathcal{K}}

acting on the same underlying 0-cells
A\in{\mathcal{O}b}{\mathcal{K}}
(that is, morphisms in the monoidal categories
{\mathcal{K}}(A,A)
for every
A
) correspond exactly
to the string diagrams in the monoidal categories
{\mathcal{K}}(A,A)
(due to the isomorphism (
1
)). This is even more clear if

{\mathcal{K}}=Del({\mathcal{C}})
for some monoidal category
{\mathcal{C}}
.

Let
{\mathcal{F}}
be a lax functor and
{\mathcal{G}}
a colax functor. We depict their lax, respectively colax structures by diagrams in the following way:

where
g,f
are composable 1-cells and
A
a 0-cell in the domain 2-category. We will often simplify the notation
\circ

for the composition of 1-cells by concatenation.

Observe that a colax transformation between two lax functors, Definition
3.2
, is nothing but a distributive law between lax functor
structures that is moreover natural in 1-cells.
Similarly, a colax transformation between two colax functors is a distributive law between colax functor structures that is moreover natural in 1-cells.
In string diagrams we may write the latter as follows:

(12)

for any 2-cell
\alpha:x\Rightarrow y:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B
.
(A colax transformation between two lax functors is determined by string diagrams that are both vertically and horizontally symmetric
to the above ones.)
Dually (taking opposite 2-cells,
i.e.
taking vertically symmetric diagrams to those in (
12
)) we obtain string diagrams
for lax transformations between two lax functors.

3.5
Adjoints in 2-categories

Dualisability plays a prominent role in the study of monoidal categories and the closely related subject of (extended) topological quantum field theories, see
[
2
]
. For example, Section 2 of
[
6
]
and Section 4 of
[
14
]
discuss and utilize ‘duals’ of bimodule categories and centers.
In the following, we want to provide a 2-categorical perspective on these constructions, thereby giving a theoretical underpinning for some of the ad-hoc constructions of
[
14
]
.

We start by briefly recalling the notion of adjoint 1-cells in 2-categories, see for example
[
13
,
19
]
.
Let
f\colon A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B
be a 1-cell in a 2-category
{\mathcal{K}}
.
A
left adjoint
of
f
is a 1-cell
u\colon B\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
together with two 2-cells
\eta:\operatorname{id}_{A}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}uf
and
\varepsilon:fu\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\operatorname{id}_{B}
such that

\frac{\operatorname{Id}_{f}\circ\eta}{\varepsilon\circ\operatorname{Id}_{f}}=\operatorname{id}_{f}\hskip 18.49988pt\hskip 18.49988pt\text{and}\hskip 18.49988pt\hskip 18.49988pt\frac{\eta\circ\operatorname{Id}_{u}}{\operatorname{Id}_{u}\circ\varepsilon}=\operatorname{id}_{u}.

Similarly, a
right adjoint
of
f
is a 1-cell
v\colon B\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
together with two 2-cells
\bar{\eta}\colon\operatorname{id}_{B}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}fv
and
\bar{\varepsilon}\colon vf\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\operatorname{id}_{A}
such that

\frac{\operatorname{Id}_{v}\circ\bar{\eta}}{\bar{\varepsilon}\circ\operatorname{Id}_{v}}=\operatorname{id}_{f}\hskip 18.49988pt\hskip 18.49988pt\text{and}\hskip 18.49988pt\hskip 18.49988pt\frac{\bar{\eta}\circ\operatorname{Id}_{f}}{f\circ\bar{\varepsilon}}=\operatorname{id}_{f}.

In string diagrams we will write
\eta=\begin{minipage}[24.0pt]\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.0){\line{1}{0}{0.0}}\put(12.0,0.0){\oval(12.0,18.0)[t]}\put(0.0,12.0){\line{1}{0}{0.0}}\end{picture}{\vskip 9.68745pt}\end{minipage}
and
\varepsilon=\begin{minipage}[24.0pt]\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.0){\line{1}{0}{0.0}}\put(12.0,12.0){\oval(12.0,18.0)[b]}\put(0.0,12.0){\line{1}{0}{0.0}}\end{picture}{\vskip 9.68745pt}\end{minipage}
, and they satisfy the laws:

\begin{minipage}[36.0pt]\begin{picture}\put(0.0,36.0){\line{1}{0}{0.0}}\put(6.0,40.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0)[b]{$\scriptstyle$}}\put(30.0,40.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0)[b]{$\scriptstyle u$}}\put(12.0,24.0){\oval(12.0,18.0)[t]}\put(30.0,36.0){\line{0}{-1}{12.0}}\put(6.0,24.0){\line{0}{-1}{12.0}}\put(24.0,24.0){\oval(12.0,18.0)[b]}\put(6.0,0.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0)[b]{$\scriptstyle u$}}\put(0.0,12.0){\line{1}{0}{0.0}}\end{picture}{\vskip 9.68745pt}\end{minipage}=\operatorname{Id}_{u}\hskip 18.49988pt\textnormal{and}\hskip 18.49988pt\begin{minipage}[36.0pt]\begin{picture}\put(0.0,36.0){\line{1}{0}{0.0}}\put(6.0,40.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0)[b]{$\scriptstyle f$}}\put(18.0,40.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0)[b]{$\scriptstyle$}}\put(6.0,36.0){\line{0}{-1}{12.0}}\put(24.0,24.0){\oval(12.0,18.0)[t]}\put(12.0,24.0){\oval(12.0,18.0)[b]}\put(30.0,24.0){\line{0}{-1}{12.0}}\put(6.0,0.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0)[b]{$\scriptstyle$}}\put(36.0,0.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0)[b]{$\scriptstyle\hskip-2.84544ptf$}}\put(0.0,12.0){\line{1}{0}{0.0}}\end{picture}{\vskip 9.68745pt}\end{minipage}=\operatorname{Id}_{f}.

Pseudofunctors
{\mathcal{F}}:{\mathcal{K}}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}{\mathcal{K}}^{\prime}
preserve (and reflect) adjoints and we have:

\begin{minipage}[36.0pt]\begin{picture}\put(0.0,12.0){\line{1}{0}{0.0}}\put(6.0,16.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0)[b]{$\scriptstyle{\mathcal{F}}(f)$}}\put(30.0,16.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0)[b]{$\scriptstyle{\mathcal{F}}(u)$}}\put(18.0,12.0){\oval(24.0,18.0)[b]}\put(0.0,12.0){\line{1}{0}{0.0}}\end{picture}{\vskip 9.68745pt}\end{minipage},\hskip 18.49988pt\begin{minipage}[36.0pt]\begin{picture}\put(0.0,12.0){\line{1}{0}{0.0}}\put(18.0,12.0){\oval(24.0,18.0)[t]}\put(6.0,0.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0)[b]{$\scriptstyle{\mathcal{F}}(u)$}}\put(30.0,0.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0)[b]{$\scriptstyle{\mathcal{F}}(f)$}}\put(0.0,12.0){\line{1}{0}{0.0}}\end{picture}{\vskip 9.68745pt}\end{minipage}=\begin{minipage}[36.0pt]\begin{picture}\put(0.0,36.0){\line{1}{0}{0.0}}\put(18.0,36.0){\line{0}{1}{6.0}}\put(18.0,42.0){\circle{5.0}}\put(1.0,35.0){\line{1}{0}{12.0}}\put(1.0,25.0){\line{1}{0}{12.0}}\put(1.0,25.0){\line{0}{1}{10.0}}\put(18.0,30.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){$\scriptstyle\hskip-9.67383pt{\mathcal{F}}(\eta)$}}\put(18.0,36.0){\line{0}{-1}{1.0}}\put(18.0,24.0){\line{0}{1}{1.0}}\put(23.0,25.0){\line{-1}{0}{12.0}}\put(23.0,35.0){\line{-1}{0}{12.0}}\put(23.0,35.0){\line{0}{-1}{10.0}}\put(18.0,12.0){\oval(24.0,18.0)[t]}\put(18.0,24.0){\line{0}{-1}{3.0}}\put(6.0,0.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0)[b]{$\scriptstyle{\mathcal{F}}(u)$}}\put(30.0,0.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0)[b]{$\scriptstyle{\mathcal{F}}(f).$}}\put(0.0,12.0){\line{1}{0}{0.0}}\end{picture}{\vskip 9.68745pt}\end{minipage}

(13)

In the aforementioned
[
14
]
, certain trace-like morphisms were considered in order to implement pivotal structures on the Drinfel‘d
centers. This involves a ‘lift’ of the notion of duals, i.e. adjoints, to the setting of centers of bimodule categories.
With our interpretation of
{\mathcal{Z}}_{l}^{w}({\mathcal{E}},{\mathcal{K}})=\operatorname{Lax}_{clx}(Del({\mathcal{E}}),{\mathcal{K}})
in Proposition
3.9
as a bicategory, for a monoidal category
{\mathcal{E}}

and a bicategory
{\mathcal{K}}
, we can now derive a more conceptual version of this construction.

Definition 3.12

We refer to a 2-category
{\mathcal{K}}
as
autonomous
if all 1-cells in
{\mathcal{K}}
have left and right adjoints.

The most prominent example of an autonomous 2-category is given by the delooping
Del({\mathcal{E}})
of an autonomous (rigid)
monoidal category
{\mathcal{E}}
. We will prove at the end of this section that the full sub-bicategory
{\mathcal{Z}}_{l}^{w-ps}({\mathcal{E}},{\mathcal{K}})

whose objects are pseudofunctors is autonomous. We will use the following fact, which is immediately proved:

Lemma 3.13

Let
{\mathcal{F}},{\mathcal{G}}:{\mathcal{K}}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}{\mathcal{K}}^{\prime}
be pseudofunctors between 2-categories, and suppose that
\chi:{\mathcal{F}}\Rightarrow{\mathcal{G}}
is a colax natural transformation
with respect to their lax functors structures. Then
\chi
is a colax natural transformation of pseudofunctors.

The following result is a 2-categorical interpretation of the fact that the half-braidings over autonomous monoidal
categories are automatically invertible, see
[
29
, Lemma 3.1]
.

Proposition 3.14

Let
{\mathcal{K}}
be an autonomous 2-category and
{\mathcal{E}}
an autonomous monoidal category. For any pair of pseudofunctors

{\mathcal{F}},{\mathcal{G}}\colon Del({\mathcal{E}})\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}{\mathcal{K}}
,
the weak and strong centers coincide. Moreover, the inverse of a left half-braiding is a right half-braiding, that is:

{\mathcal{Z}}_{l}^{w-ps}({\mathcal{F}},{\mathcal{K}},{\mathcal{G}})={\mathcal{Z}}_{l}^{s-ps}({\mathcal{F}},{\mathcal{K}},{\mathcal{G}})\cong{\mathcal{Z}}_{r}^{s-ps}({\mathcal{F}},{\mathcal{K}},{\mathcal{G}})={\mathcal{Z}}_{r}^{w-ps}({\mathcal{F}},{\mathcal{K}},{\mathcal{G}})
.

Proof.

For the first claim it suffices to show that the component 2-cells of any colax natural transformation between pseudofunctors
\chi\colon{\mathcal{F}}\Rightarrow{\mathcal{G}}
are invertible.
Hereto, we fix an object
X\in{\mathcal{E}}
and write
X^{\ast}
for its left adjoint.
Starting from the left hand-side below and applying the left equality in (
13
),
the coherence of
\chi
with the functor’s
multiplicativity, naturality of
\chi
with respect to
\varepsilon_{X}
and the coherence of
\chi
with respect to the functor’s counitalities
(mind Lemma
3.13
) we reach the equality with the right hand-side:

\begin{minipage}[48.0pt]\begin{picture}\put(0.0,48.0){\line{1}{0}{0.0}}\put(0.0,52.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0)[b]{$\scriptstyle D_{\chi}$}}\put(0.0,12.0){\line{1}{0}{0.0}}\end{picture}{\vskip 9.68745pt}\end{minipage}=\begin{minipage}[36.0pt]\begin{picture}\put(0.0,48.0){\line{1}{0}{0.0}}\put(6.0,52.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0)[b]{$\scriptstyle D_{\chi}$}}\put(24.0,52.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0)[b]{$\scriptstyle{\mathcal{F}}(X)$}}\put(48.0,52.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0)[b]{$\scriptstyle{\mathcal{F}}(X^{*})$}}\put(6.0,48.0){\line{0}{-1}{36.0}}\put(30.0,48.0){\oval(24.0,18.0)[b]}\put(6.0,0.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0)[b]{$\scriptstyle D_{\chi}.$}}\put(0.0,12.0){\line{1}{0}{0.0}}\end{picture}{\vskip 9.68745pt}\end{minipage}

Then the 2-cell:

\gamma_{X}:=\begin{minipage}[48.0pt]\begin{picture}\put(0.0,48.0){\line{1}{0}{0.0}}\put(0.0,52.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0)[b]{$\scriptstyle{\mathcal{G}}(X)$}}\put(18.0,52.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0)[b]{$\scriptstyle D_{\chi}$}}\put(6.0,48.0){\line{0}{-1}{24.0}}\put(18.0,48.0){\line{0}{-1}{12.0}}\put(36.0,36.0){\oval(12.0,18.0)[t]}\put(18.0,36.0){\line{0}{-1}{1.0}}\put(18.0,24.0){\line{0}{1}{1.0}}\put(13.0,25.0){\line{1}{0}{12.0}}\put(13.0,35.0){\line{1}{0}{12.0}}\put(13.0,35.0){\line{0}{-1}{10.0}}\put(30.0,30.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){$\scriptstyle\hskip-9.67383pt\chi_{X^{*}}$}}\put(30.0,36.0){\line{0}{-1}{1.0}}\put(30.0,24.0){\line{0}{1}{1.0}}\put(35.0,25.0){\line{-1}{0}{12.0}}\put(35.0,35.0){\line{-1}{0}{12.0}}\put(35.0,35.0){\line{0}{-1}{10.0}}\put(42.0,36.0){\line{0}{-1}{24.0}}\put(12.0,24.0){\oval(12.0,18.0)[b]}\put(30.0,24.0){\line{0}{-1}{12.0}}\put(30.0,0.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0)[b]{$\scriptstyle D_{\chi}$}}\put(48.0,0.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0)[b]{$\scriptstyle{\mathcal{F}}(X)$}}\put(0.0,12.0){\line{1}{0}{0.0}}\end{picture}{\vskip 9.68745pt}\end{minipage}

is clearly a left inverse of
\chi_{X}:D_{\chi}\circ{\mathcal{F}}(X)\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}{\mathcal{G}}(X)\circ D_{\chi}
. The analogous reasoning, but this time using the coherence
of
\chi
with the comultiplicativity and unitality of the functors, shows that
\gamma_{X}
is also a right inverse of
\chi_{X}
.
The last statement is proved directly.
∎∎

Our next result states that adjoints can be ‘lifted’ to weak centers.

Proposition 3.15

Suppose
{\mathcal{E}}
to be an autonomous monoidal category and
{\mathcal{K}}
to be an autonomous 2-category.
Then the full sub-bicategory
{\mathcal{Z}}^{w\mbox{-}ps}_{l}({\mathcal{E}},{\mathcal{K}})\subset{\mathcal{Z}}_{l}^{w}({\mathcal{E}},{\mathcal{K}})
whose objects are pseudofunctors is autonomous.

Proof.

We only show that any 1-cell in
{\mathcal{Z}}^{w\mbox{-}ps}_{l}({\mathcal{E}},{\mathcal{K}})
, that is, an object

(M,\chi)\in{\mathcal{Z}}^{w\mbox{-}ps}_{l}({\mathcal{F}},{\mathcal{K}},{\mathcal{G}})
, has a right adjoint living in
{\mathcal{Z}}^{w\mbox{-}ps}_{l}({\mathcal{G}},{\mathcal{K}},{\mathcal{F}})
.
The case of left adjoints is analogous.
Due to Proposition
3.14
, the half-braiding
\chi
,
i.e.
the 2-cells
\chi_{X}:M\circ{\mathcal{F}}(X)\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}{\mathcal{G}}(X)\circ M
, is invertible.
We utilize this fact, to define a half-braiding on
{}^{\ast}M
as shown in the next diagram

\sigma_{X}:=\begin{minipage}[48.0pt]\begin{picture}\put(0.0,48.0){\line{1}{0}{0.0}}\put(0.0,52.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0)[b]{$\scriptstyle{}^{\ast}M$}}\put(18.0,52.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0)[b]{$\scriptstyle{\mathcal{G}}(X)$}}\put(6.0,48.0){\line{0}{-1}{24.0}}\put(18.0,48.0){\line{0}{-1}{12.0}}\put(36.0,36.0){\oval(12.0,18.0)[t]}\put(18.0,36.0){\line{0}{-1}{1.0}}\put(18.0,24.0){\line{0}{1}{1.0}}\put(13.0,25.0){\line{1}{0}{12.0}}\put(13.0,35.0){\line{1}{0}{12.0}}\put(13.0,35.0){\line{0}{-1}{10.0}}\put(30.0,30.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){$\scriptstyle\hskip-9.67383pt\chi_{X}^{-1}$}}\put(30.0,36.0){\line{0}{-1}{1.0}}\put(30.0,24.0){\line{0}{1}{1.0}}\put(35.0,25.0){\line{-1}{0}{12.0}}\put(35.0,35.0){\line{-1}{0}{12.0}}\put(35.0,35.0){\line{0}{-1}{10.0}}\put(42.0,36.0){\line{0}{-1}{24.0}}\put(12.0,24.0){\oval(12.0,18.0)[b]}\put(30.0,24.0){\line{0}{-1}{12.0}}\put(30.0,0.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0)[b]{$\scriptstyle{\mathcal{F}}(X)$}}\put(48.0,0.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0)[b]{$\scriptstyle{}^{\ast}M.$}}\put(0.0,12.0){\line{1}{0}{0.0}}\end{picture}{\vskip 9.68745pt}\end{minipage}

A direct computation shows that it satisfies the Equations (2) and (3). For example, we have

To conclude the proof, we show that the unit
\eta\colon\operatorname{id}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}M\circ{}^{\ast}M
and counit
\varepsilon\colon{}^{\ast}M\circ M\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\operatorname{id}
lift to
morphisms in the center category
{\mathcal{Z}}^{w\mbox{-}ps}_{l}({\mathcal{G}},{\mathcal{K}},{\mathcal{F}})
. For the counit this follows from the computation depicted below.

An analogous argument shows that the unit also becomes a morphism in the center.
∎

4
Bilax functors

We are interested in functors on bicategories that are both lax and colax but not necessarily pseudofunctors.
Likewise, we are interested in natural transformations that are both lax and colax but not necessarily pseudonatural
transformations, as well as in their modifications. In particular, we introduce the notions of a bilax functor, bilax
natural transformation and bilax modifications. We formulate them for 2-categories, just to avoid the use of associators and unitors,
but the corresponding definitions for bicategories can be formulated in a straightforward fashion.
To that end, we fix 2-categories
{\mathcal{K}}
and
{\mathcal{K}}^{\prime}
. In this section we introduce bilax functors, and leave the resting two
notions for the next section.

4.1
Bilax functors

We reiterate that we will often simplify the notation
\circ
for the horizontal composition of 1- and 2-cells by concatenation.

Definition 4.1

Let
{\mathcal{F}}:{\mathcal{K}}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}{\mathcal{K}}^{\prime}
be a 2-functor. A
Yang-Baxter operator
for
{\mathcal{F}}
consists of a collection of 2-cells
\nu_{g,f}:{\mathcal{F}}(g){\mathcal{F}}(f)\Rightarrow{\mathcal{F}}(f){\mathcal{F}}(g)
in
{\mathcal{K}}^{\prime}
, natural in 1-endocells
f,g
of
{\mathcal{K}}
,
which satisfy
the Yang-Baxter equation

(15)

We call a Yang-Baxter operator for the identity 2-functor
\operatorname{Id}:{\mathcal{K}}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}{\mathcal{K}}

a Yang-Baxter operator of
{\mathcal{K}}
.
We reserve the notation
c
for a Yang-Baxter operator on the identity 2-functor on
{\mathcal{K}}
.

For 2-categories
{\mathcal{K}}=Del({\mathcal{C}})
where
{\mathcal{C}}
is a braided monoidal category, a class of Yang-Baxter operators
c
is given by the braiding(s) of
{\mathcal{C}}
.

Definition 4.2

Assume that
{\mathcal{K}}
possesses a Yang-Baxter operator
c
. A
bilax functor
is a pair
({\mathcal{F}},\nu):({\mathcal{K}},c)\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}{\mathcal{K}}^{\prime}

where
{\mathcal{F}}:{\mathcal{K}}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}{\mathcal{K}}^{\prime}
is simultaneously
a lax and a colax functor and
\nu
is a Yang-Baxter operator of
{\mathcal{F}}
,
meaning that apart from the rule (
14
) two additional groups of rules hold: left and right lax distributive laws

). We record that the unit laws of (

9.68745pt}\end{minipage}

(19)

We briefly comment the term “distributive law” in equtions (
16
) and (
17
) (we also used it in (
12
)).
This term in the context of lax functors appeared in
[
9
, Definition 3.1]
, as the authors say: “by
analogy to the distributive laws of monads, which have similar axioms”. On the other hand, monads and lax monoidal functors
on (monoidal) categories can both be
interpreted as monoids: the former are monoids in endofunctor categories, while the latter are monoids under Day convolution.
In accordance with this suggestive similarity we use the term “distributive law” also for pseudonatural transformations on lax/colax/bilax functors.

Let
({\mathcal{K}},c)
and
({\mathcal{K}}^{\prime},d)
be 2-categories with their respective Yang-Baxter operators, and assume that a bilax functor

({\mathcal{F}},\nu)
is given acting between them. If
\nu_{f,g}=d_{{\mathcal{F}}(f),{\mathcal{F}}(g)}
for all composable 1-endocells
f,g
in
{\mathcal{K}}
, we will
say that
{\mathcal{F}}
is a bilax functor with a
compatible Yang-Baxter operator
, and we will write simply
{\mathcal{F}}:({\mathcal{K}},c)\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}({\mathcal{K}}^{\prime},d)
.
In the case that the relation between
\nu
and
d
is not known, we will write
({\mathcal{F}},\nu):({\mathcal{K}},c)\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}{\mathcal{K}}^{\prime}
, as in Definition
4.2
.

Given a bilax functor
({\mathcal{F}},\nu)
the functors on endo-hom-categories

{\mathcal{F}}_{A,A}:{\mathcal{K}}(A,A)\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}{\mathcal{K}}^{\prime}({\mathcal{F}}(A),{\mathcal{F}}(A))

(20)

are pre-bimonoidal in the sense of
[
24
, Section 2]
,
where we rely on the strictification theorem for monoidal categories. If instead of the Yang-Baxter operators one works
with braidings on the endo-hom categories, then the functors
{\mathcal{F}}_{A,A}
are bilax as in
[
1
, Section 19.9.1]
.
The latter inspired the terminology in Definition
4.2
.
While the analogues of the coherence conditions (
16
) and (
17
) do not appear explicitly in
[
24
, Section 2]
, we incorporate them in accordance with the discussion in
[
1
, Section 19.9.1]
in our definition. Accordingly,
we recover a “braided bialgebra” from
[
31
, Definition 5.1]
, as we will show further below. In the situation

{\mathcal{F}}:({\mathcal{K}},c)\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}({\mathcal{K}}^{\prime},d)
(
i.e.
that
\nu
is compatible with
d
), the functors
{\mathcal{F}}_{A,A}
are bimonoidal in the sense of

[
24
, Section 2]
. The other way around, we clearly have:

Example 4.3

Let
({\mathcal{C}},\Phi_{\mathcal{C}})
and
({\mathcal{D}},\Phi_{\mathcal{D}})
be braided monoidal categories. We identify their braidings with Yang-Baxter operators
c
and
d

on their delooping categories
Del({\mathcal{C}})
and
Del({\mathcal{D}})
, respectively. Any bilax functor
{\mathcal{F}}:(Del({\mathcal{C}}),c)\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}(Del({\mathcal{C}}),d)
with a compatible Yang-Baxter operator is a bimonoidal functor in the sense of
[
24
, Section 2]
.

Example 4.4

Let
({\mathcal{C}},\Phi)
be a braided monoidal category and
1
the trivial 2-category (it has a single 0-cell and only
identity higher cells). Any bilax functor
{\mathcal{F}}:1\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}Del({\mathcal{C}})
with invertible
\nu

which coincided with
\Phi
can be identified with a bialgebra in
{\mathcal{C}}
. To see this, note that
{\mathcal{F}}
determines and is determined by a 1-cell in
Del({\mathcal{C}})
, i.e. an object
B
of
{\mathcal{C}}
, and a (co-)multiplication and (co-)unit on that 1-cell, which are subject to (co-)associativity and (co-)unitality due to
{\mathcal{F}}
being lax and colax. On the other hand,
c
of the trivial 2-category is trivial:
it is the identity 2-cell on the identity 1-cell on
*
. Then the equations (
18
) recover bialgebra axioms on
{\mathcal{F}}(id_{*})
. (The rest of axioms of the bilax functor are automatically fulfilled by the braiding,
and do not contribute any additional information.)

If moreover
{\mathcal{F}}
is a pseudofunctor, then it is trivial: the obtained bialgebra is isomorphic to the monoidal unit of
{\mathcal{C}}
.
This can be proved considering the lax unitality structure of
{\mathcal{F}}
.

The following result is straightforwardly proved, see also
[
24
, Proposition 3.9]
.

Proposition 4.5

Let
{\mathcal{F}}:({\mathcal{K}},c)\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}({\mathcal{K}}^{\prime},d)
and
{\mathcal{G}}:({\mathcal{K}}^{\prime},d)\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}({\mathcal{K}}^{\prime\prime},e)
be compatible bilax functors.
Then
{\mathcal{G}}{\mathcal{F}}:({\mathcal{K}},c)\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}({\mathcal{K}}^{\prime\prime},e)
is a compatible bilax functor with a Yang-Baxter operator
\nu_{g,f}:=\nu^{\mathcal{G}}_{{\mathcal{F}}(g),{\mathcal{F}}(f)}
,
where
\nu^{\mathcal{G}}
is a Yang-Baxter operator of
{\mathcal{G}}
and
g,f
are 1-endocells of
{\mathcal{K}}
.

4.2
Bimonads

The theory of monads and comonads in the context of 2-categories was introduced by Street in
[
30
]
. Recall that a monad in
{\mathcal{K}}
, is
a 1-endo-cell
t\in{\mathcal{K}}(A,A)
endowed with 2-cells
\mu:tt\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}t
and
\eta:id_{A}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}t
subject to associativity and unitality conditions.
Dually, that is, swapping the direction of the structure 2-cells, one obtains the notion of a comonad in
{\mathcal{K}}
: a 1-endo-cell endowed with a coassociative and counital comultiplication. In order to differentiate notations for (co)lax functors and (co)monad structures,
we will represent the multiplication and unit of a monad as well as the comultiplication and counit of a comonad by:

and a colax functor
{\mathcal{G}}

we have the following monad and comonad structures:

(21)

We are going to introduce bimonads in 2-categories with respect to Yang-Baxter operators.
Observe that their 1-categorical analogue is different than the bimonads of
[
26
]
and
[
27
]
in ordinary categories,
but they are a particular instance of
\tau
-bimonads and bimonads in 2-categories from
[
11
]
. Whereas bimonads in
[
27
]

are opmonoidal monads on monoidal categories, bimonads in
[
26
]
are monads and comonads on a not necessarily monoidal category
with compatibility conditions that involve a distributive law
\lambda
. The bimonads in
[
11
, Definition 7.1]
generalize the latter
to 2-categories and are equipped with an analogous 2-cell
i.e.
distributive law
\lambda
. The
\tau
-bimonads that we also introduced in
[
11
]
, are a particular case of bimonads, where the 2-cell
\lambda
is given in terms of a 2-cell
\tau
which is a distributive law both on the left and on the right, both with respect to monads and comonads.
In the special case of a Yang-Baxter operator coming from a braiding one gets examples à la
[
26
]
.

Definition 4.6

Let
({\mathcal{K}},c)
be a 2-category with a Yang-Baxter operator
c
and suppose that
b\in{\mathcal{K}}
is a 1-endo-cell endowed with a monad and comonad structure.
We call
b
a
c
-bimonad
if
c_{b,b}
is a distributive law both on the left and on the right, both with respect to monads and comonads,
and the following compatibilities hold:

(22)

The following observation is inspired by Benaboú:

Lemma 4.7

There exists a bijection between
c
-bimonads in
({\mathcal{K}},c)
and compatible bilax functors
1\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}({\mathcal{K}},c)
.
It is given by mapping any
c
-bimonad
b:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
in
({\mathcal{K}},c)
to the bilax functor
{\mathcal{T}}:1\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}{\mathcal{K}}
with
{\mathcal{T}}(id_{*})=b
, whose bilax structure 2-cells agree with the respective 2-cells of the
c
-bimonad
b
.

Proof.

As observed by Benaboú, a lax functor from
1
to
{\mathcal{K}}
defines and is defined by a monad in
{\mathcal{K}}
.
Likewise, the colaxity of
{\mathcal{T}}:1\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}{\mathcal{K}}
is equivalent to
{\mathcal{T}}(id_{*})
being a comonad. The equations (
16
) and
(
17
) correspond to the four distributive laws of
c_{b,b}
with
b={\mathcal{T}}(id_{*})
and the bilaxity conditions given in (
18
)
correspond to conditions (
22
). Such a bilax functor is also a
\tau
-bimonad in the sense of
[
11
, Definition 7.1]
(it is a monad and a comonad with
a monad-morphic and a comonad-morphic distributive law on both sides, in the sense of (
16
) and (
17
)).
∎∎

Similarly, we clearly have:

Lemma 4.8

Any bilax functor
({\mathcal{T}},\nu):1\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}{\mathcal{K}}
determines a
\nu
-bimonad
{\mathcal{T}}(id_{*})=b
.

Proposition 4.9

Let
({\mathcal{F}},\nu):({\mathcal{K}},c)\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}{\mathcal{K}}^{\prime}
be a bilax functor and
b\in{\mathcal{K}}
a
c
-bimonad. Then
{\mathcal{F}}(b)
is a
\nu
-bimonad in
{\mathcal{K}}^{\prime}
.
That is, it satisfies the last three axioms of (
22
) and a variant of the first axiom shown below:

(23)

Moreover,
{\mathcal{F}}(b)
is a
\tau
-bimonad in the sense of
[
11
]
.

Proof.

The claim follows by the naturality of the (co)lax structure of
{\mathcal{F}}
with respect to the 2-cells from the (co)monad structures of
b
.
(For the three compatibilities of the (co)unit structures for
{\mathcal{F}}(b)
, alternatively, apply
{\mathcal{F}}(\eta_{b})
and
{\mathcal{F}}(\varepsilon_{b})
at suitable places in the last three equations in (
18
) and use the naturality of the (co)lax structure of
{\mathcal{F}}
.)
The last statement follows from (
16
) and (
17
).
∎∎

Example 4.10

We have that
{\mathcal{F}}(id_{A})
for all
A\in{\mathcal{O}b}{\mathcal{K}}
are
\nu
-bimonads in
{\mathcal{K}}^{\prime}
.

Given that the notion of a Yang-Baxter operator is more general than that of
a braiding, a
\nu
-bimonad generalizes the notion of a bialgebra. Moreover, we have that the notion of a bilax functor
{\mathcal{F}}:{\mathcal{K}}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}{\mathcal{K}}^{\prime}

recovers that of a “braided bialgebra” from
[
31
, Definition 5.1]
, in the case
{\mathcal{K}}=Del({\mathcal{C}})
, where
{\mathcal{C}}
is a monoidal category with a single object.

Example 4.11

Let
({\mathcal{K}},c)
and
({\mathcal{K}}^{\prime},d)
be two 2-categories with their respective Yang-Baxter operators, and let
b:A_{0}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A_{0}
be a
d
-bimonad in
{\mathcal{K}}^{\prime}
.
We define
{\mathcal{F}}_{b}(A)=A_{0},{\mathcal{F}}_{b}(x)=b,{\mathcal{F}}_{b}(\alpha)=\operatorname{Id}_{b}
for all objects
A
, 1-cells
x
and 2-cells
\alpha
in
{\mathcal{K}}
.
Then
{\mathcal{F}}_{b}
is a bilax functor with
\nu_{f,g}=d_{b,b}
for all 1-endocells
f,g
in
{\mathcal{K}}
.
(Alternatively, instead of the Yang-Baxter operator
d
in
{\mathcal{K}}^{\prime}
one could require
a Yang-Baxter operator
\nu
on
{\mathcal{F}}_{b}
, take a
\nu
-bimonad
b
and obtain a bilax functor (
{\mathcal{F}}_{b},\nu)
.)

The following claim is directly proved:

\chi:{\mathcal{F}}\Rightarrow{\mathcal{G}}

Monads and comonads in
{\mathcal{K}}
can act on other 1-cells of
{\mathcal{K}}
. That is, for example a left module over a bimonad
b:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
is a 1-cell

x:A^{\prime}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
endowed with a left action
\rhd:bx\Rightarrow x
of
b
(in
[
10
, Definition 2.3]
the axioms are expressed in string diagrams). Note that in category theory, what we call a
b
-(co)module is also referred to as a
b
-(co)algebra, see for example
[
20
]
.

Proposition 4.13

Let
{\mathcal{F}},{\mathcal{G}}
be two bilax functors and
b:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
a
c
-bimonad in
{\mathcal{K}}
(indeed, a monad and comonad
satisfying the two last identities in (
22
)).

For a colax transformation
\phi:{\mathcal{F}}\Rightarrow{\mathcal{G}}
of colax functors, (
24
) defines a left
{\mathcal{G}}(b)
-comodule structure
on
\phi(A)
.

Dually, for a lax transformation
\psi:{\mathcal{F}}\Rightarrow{\mathcal{G}}
of lax functors, (
25
) defines a left
{\mathcal{G}}(b)
-module structure
on
\psi(A)
.
