(25)

Proof.

We indicate the steps of the proof for the comodule structure. Starting from

apply first the second rule in (
18
), then the first rule in (
12
), naturality of

, naturality of
\chi
, third rule in (
22
) for
b
, and lastly naturality of
\chi
.

For the counitality, starting from

apply first the fourth rule in (
22
), the second rule in (
12
), and lastly the fourth
rule in (
18
).
∎∎

For
{\mathcal{F}}={\mathcal{G}}
acting on the trivial 2-category
{\mathcal{K}}=1
, and
\phi
and
\psi
being endo-transformations, Proposition
4.13
recovers

[
10
, Proposition 2.4]
, proved for 2-(co)monads and their distributive laws. The latter is a 2-categorical formulation for a fact
possibly used in (braided) monoidal categories by different authors, but we are not aware of an exact reference.

It is important to note that modules/comodules over a
c
-bimonad in
{\mathcal{K}}
do not form a monoidal category (unless the Yang-Baxter operator
c
is a half-braiding).

4.3
Module comonads, comodule monads and relative bimonad modules

In
[
20
]
the notion of a wreath was introduced as monad in the free completion 2-category
{\rm EM}^{M}({\mathcal{K}})
of the 2-category
{\rm Mnd}({\mathcal{K}})

of monads in
{\mathcal{K}}
under the Eilenberg-Moore construction. Dually, cowreaths are comonads in
{\rm EM}^{C}({\mathcal{K}})
, where the latter is the analogous
completion of the 2-category
{\rm Comnd}({\mathcal{K}})
of comonads. In
[
10
]
the first author introduced the 2-category
{\rm bEM}({\mathcal{K}})

so that there are forgetful 2-functors
{\rm bEM}({\mathcal{K}})\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}{\rm EM}^{M}({\mathcal{K}})
and
{\rm bEM}({\mathcal{K}})\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}{\rm EM}^{C}({\mathcal{K}})
. Moreover, in
loc. cit.
biwreaths
were introduced as bimonads in
{\rm bEM}({\mathcal{K}})
. Biwreaths as a notion integrate both
wreaths and cowreaths as well as their mixed versions: mixed wreaths and mixed cowreaths. In particular, a biwreath also behaves like a
“(co)module (co)monad” with respect to monad-morphic or comonad-morphic distributive law in
{\mathcal{K}}
, where the highlighted notions in the
2-categorical setting were introduced in
[
10
]
.

In the present paper, similarly to the above-mentioned idea (see diagrams (67) and (65) of
loc. cit.
), but now with respect to Yang-Baxter operators,
we will consider the notions that we introduce in the definition below.

For the sake of examples that we will study further below, we record that in
[
10
]
the 2-category
{\rm Bimnd}({\mathcal{K}})
of bimonads in
{\mathcal{K}}
(with
respect to distributive laws) was defined, so that there are inclusion and projection 2-functors
E_{B}:{\rm Bimnd}({\mathcal{K}})\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}{\rm bEM}({\mathcal{K}})
and

\pi:{\rm bEM}({\mathcal{K}})\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}{\rm Bimnd}({\mathcal{K}})
which are identities on 0- and 1-cells. In
[
11
]
we have considered a variation of the 2-category
{\rm Bimnd}({\mathcal{K}})

by changing the definition of 2-cells. It is this other version of the 2-category that we will be interested in here. We will recall it in Example
5.2
.

Definition 4.14

Let
b:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
be a
c
-bimonad in a 2-category
({\mathcal{K}},c)
with a Yang-Baxter operator. Let
d:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
be a comonad and a right
b
-module,
and
t:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
a monad and a right
b
-module.
We say that
d
is a (right)
module comonad
if the left two equations below hold,
and that
t
is a (right)
comodule monad
if the right two equations below hold:

in

The analogous claims hold on left sides.

Proposition 4.15

Bilax functors
{\mathcal{F}}:({\mathcal{K}},c)\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}({\mathcal{K}}^{\prime},d)
with compatible Yang-Baxter operators preserve module comonads and comodule monads.

Proof.

The arguments for showing that
{\mathcal{F}}
preserves comodule monads are analogous to proving that it maps module comonads to module comonads.
Therefore, we will restrict ourselves to the latter. Let
b:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
be a bimonad in
({\mathcal{K}},c)
and
x:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
a
b
-module comonad.
The bilaxity of
{\mathcal{F}}
implies that
{\mathcal{F}}(b)
is a
d
-bimonad with an action on the comonad
{\mathcal{F}}(x)
.
Hence, we only have to show that the first two compatibility conditions in Definition
4.14
hold.
Using the functoriality of
{\mathcal{F}}
we have:

The compatibility of the action of
{\mathcal{F}}(b)
with the counit of
{\mathcal{F}}(x)
is an immediate consequence of
{\mathcal{F}}
being lax.
∎∎

For the next property we introduce the following notion:

Definition 4.16

Let
b:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
be a
c
-bimonad in a 2-category
({\mathcal{K}},c)
with a Yang-Baxter operator and let
t:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
be a right
b
-comodule monad.
A right
t
-module and a right
b
-comodule
x:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B
is a right
relative
t\mbox{-}b
-module
if the following relation holds:

\begin{minipage}[48.0pt]\begin{picture}\put(0.0,48.0){\line{1}{0}{0.0}}\put(6.0,52.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0)[b]{$\scriptstyle t$}}\put(18.0,36.0){\line{0}{1}{4.0}}\put(18.0,40.0){\line{-1}{0}{12.0}}\put(18.0,40.0){\line{-3}{1}{12.0}}\put(6.0,36.0){\line{0}{1}{12.0}}\put(42.0,36.0){\line{0}{1}{4.0}}\put(42.0,40.0){\line{-1}{0}{12.0}}\put(42.0,40.0){\line{-3}{1}{12.0}}\put(30.0,36.0){\line{0}{1}{12.0}}\put(6.0,36.0){\line{0}{-1}{12.0}}\put(18.0,36.0){\line{0}{-1}{1.0}}\put(18.0,24.0){\line{0}{1}{1.0}}\put(13.0,25.0){\line{1}{0}{12.0}}\put(13.0,35.0){\line{1}{0}{12.0}}\put(13.0,35.0){\line{0}{-1}{10.0}}\put(30.0,30.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){$\scriptstyle\hskip-9.67383ptc_{b,t}$}}\put(30.0,36.0){\line{0}{-1}{1.0}}\put(30.0,24.0){\line{0}{1}{1.0}}\put(35.0,25.0){\line{-1}{0}{12.0}}\put(35.0,35.0){\line{-1}{0}{12.0}}\put(35.0,35.0){\line{0}{-1}{10.0}}\put(42.0,36.0){\line{0}{-1}{12.0}}\put(18.0,24.0){\line{0}{-1}{4.0}}\put(18.0,20.0){\line{-1}{0}{12.0}}\put(18.0,20.0){\line{-3}{-1}{12.0}}\put(6.0,24.0){\line{0}{-1}{12.0}}\put(36.0,24.0){\oval(12.0,18.0)[b]}\put(36.0,15.0){\line{0}{-2}{3.0}}\put(36.0,15.0){\circle*{4.0}}\put(6.0,0.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0)[b]{$\scriptstyle x$}}\put(36.0,0.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0)[b]{$\scriptstyle b$}}\put(0.0,12.0){\line{1}{0}{0.0}}\end{picture}{\vskip 9.68745pt}\end{minipage}=\begin{minipage}[36.0pt]\begin{picture}\put(0.0,60.0){\line{1}{0}{0.0}}\put(6.0,64.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0)[b]{$\scriptstyle x$}}\put(18.0,64.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0)[b]{$\scriptstyle t$}}\put(6.0,60.0){\line{0}{-1}{12.0}}\put(18.0,60.0){\line{0}{-1}{12.0}}\put(18.0,48.0){\line{0}{-1}{4.0}}\put(18.0,44.0){\line{-1}{0}{12.0}}\put(18.0,44.0){\line{-3}{-1}{12.0}}\put(6.0,48.0){\line{0}{-1}{12.0}}\put(18.0,24.0){\line{0}{1}{4.0}}\put(18.0,28.0){\line{-1}{0}{12.0}}\put(18.0,28.0){\line{-3}{1}{12.0}}\put(6.0,24.0){\line{0}{1}{12.0}}\put(6.0,24.0){\line{0}{-1}{12.0}}\put(18.0,24.0){\line{0}{-1}{12.0}}\put(6.0,0.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0)[b]{$\scriptstyle x$}}\put(18.0,0.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0)[b]{$\scriptstyle b.$}}\put(0.0,12.0){\line{1}{0}{0.0}}\end{picture}{\vskip 9.68745pt}\end{minipage}

Morphisms of right relative
t\mbox{-}b
-modules are right
t
-linear and right
b
-colinear 2-cells in
{\mathcal{K}}
.
Analogously, one defines a left
relative
t\mbox{-}b
-module
. If
t=b
we call
x
a
relative bimonad module
.

The above notions correspond to those of relative Hopf modules
[
5
]
and Hopf modules
[
22
]
in braided monoidal categories,
which in turn are categorifications of Hopf modules introduced in
[
21
]
.
Obviously,
b
itself is a relative bimonad module.

Example 4.17

Let
{\mathcal{F}}:({\mathcal{K}},c)\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}({\mathcal{K}}^{\prime},d)
be a bilax functor with compatible Yang-Baxter operator.
For any 1-cell
x:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B
the 1-cell
{\mathcal{F}}(x)
is a left relative bimonad module over
{\mathcal{F}}(id_{B})
and a right relative bimonad module over
{\mathcal{F}}(id_{A})
.
This follows from the first equation in (
18
) by setting
id_{B},id_{B},id_{B},x
, respectively
x,id_{A},id_{A},id_{A}
for the 1-cells

g,f,h,k
.

Analogously to Proposition
4.15
we get the following result:

Proposition 4.18

Bilax functors
{\mathcal{F}}:({\mathcal{K}},c)\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}({\mathcal{K}}^{\prime},d)
with compatible Yang-Baxter operators preserve relative bimonad modules.

Hopf bimodules, for Hopf algebras over a field, appeared in the construction of bicovariant differential calculi over a Hopf algebra in

[
33
]
. They were generalized in
[
4
, Section 4.2]
to the context of a braided monoidal category
{\mathcal{C}}
. For a bialgebra
B
in
{\mathcal{C}}

a Hopf bimodule is a
B
-bimodule
M
in
{\mathcal{C}}
which is moreover a
B
-bicomodule in the monoidal category of
B
-bimodules
{}_{B}{\mathcal{C}}_{B}

(for the structures on
B
itself the regular (co)action on
B
and the diagonal action on tensor product of comodules are used).
The latter means
that both left and right
B
-comodule structures of
M
are left and right
B
-bimodule morphisms, meaning that there are four conditions to be
fulfilled. Together with simultaneously
B
-linear and
B
-bicolinear morphisms Hopf bimodules make a category denoted by
{}^{B}_{B}{\mathcal{C}}_{B}^{B}
.
We mark that the name “Hopf bimodules” is somewhat misleading, as the Hopf structure on the bialgebra
B
is not
necessary here.

Substituting a braided monoidal category
{\mathcal{C}}
and a bialgebra
B
in it with a monoidal category with a Yang-Baxter operator
c
and a

c
-bimonad in it, we can consider the analogous category of Hopf bimodules
{}^{B}_{B}({\mathcal{C}},c)_{B}^{B}
, where
{\mathcal{C}}
and
B
now have these new meanings.

Corollary 4.19

Let
{\mathcal{F}}:({\mathcal{K}},c)\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}({\mathcal{K}}^{\prime},d)
be a bilax functor with compatible Yang-Baxter operator. The functors (
20
) for every
A\in{\mathcal{O}b}{\mathcal{K}}
factor through the category of Hopf bimodules over the
d
-bimonads
{\mathcal{F}}(id_{A})

in
({\mathcal{K}}^{\prime}({\mathcal{F}}(A),{\mathcal{F}}(A)),d)
.

Proof.

For any 1-endocell
x:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
we should check that
{\mathcal{F}}(x)
satisfies the four relations. Two of them are satisfied by Example
4.17
,
which mean that the left coaction is left linear and that right coaction is right linear. The other two, meaning the two mixed versions of compatibilities,
one gets by setting
x=f
, respectively
x=h
, and the resting three 1-cells to be identities, in the first equation in (
18
).

To check the claim on morphisms, observe that for any 2-cell
\alpha:x\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}y
in
{\mathcal{K}}
,
i.e.
morphism in
{\mathcal{K}}(A,A)
,

{\mathcal{F}}(\alpha)
is
{\mathcal{F}}(id_{A})
-(co)linear by the naturality of the (co)lax structure of
{\mathcal{F}}
.
∎∎

We record here some direct consequences for a bilax functor
({\mathcal{F}},\nu)
from the first equation in (
18
).
For simplicity, we may consider
{\mathcal{F}}:({\mathcal{K}},c)\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}({\mathcal{K}}^{\prime},d)
to have a compatible Yang-Baxter operator.
By (
26
) note that
{\mathcal{F}}(x)
is a bi(co)module over
{\mathcal{F}}(id)
for any 1-cell
x
(acting among suitable 0-cells).
Then we may write:

for 1-cells

5
2-category of bilax functors

In this section we introduce the rest of the ingredients to construct a 2-category of bilax functors.

5.1
Bilax natural transformations

Among bilax functors we introduce bilax natural transformations. Recall that
for a lax transformation
\psi
and a colax transformation
\phi
, both acting between (bilax) functors
{\mathcal{F}}\Rightarrow{\mathcal{F}}^{\prime}
,
for every 1-cell
f:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B
there are 2-cells

\psi_{f}:{\mathcal{F}}^{\prime}_{A,B}(f)\circ\psi(A)\Rightarrow\psi(B)\circ{\mathcal{F}}_{A,B}(f)

and

\phi_{f}:\phi(B)\circ{\mathcal{F}}_{A,B}(f)\Rightarrow{\mathcal{F}}^{\prime}_{A,B}(f)\circ\phi(A)

natural in
f
. For the sake of the following definition we introduce the notation:

\lambda_{xy,z}:=\begin{minipage}[48.0pt]\begin{picture}\put(0.0,48.0){\line{1}{0}{0.0}}\put(12.0,52.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0)[b]{$\scriptstyle{\mathcal{F}}(xy)$}}\put(36.0,52.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0)[b]{$\scriptstyle{\mathcal{F}}(z)$}}\put(12.0,36.0){\oval(12.0,18.0)[t]}\put(12.0,48.0){\line{0}{-1}{3.0}}\put(30.0,48.0){\line{0}{-1}{12.0}}\put(6.0,36.0){\line{0}{-1}{12.0}}\put(18.0,36.0){\line{0}{-1}{1.0}}\put(18.0,24.0){\line{0}{1}{1.0}}\put(13.0,25.0){\line{1}{0}{12.0}}\put(13.0,35.0){\line{1}{0}{12.0}}\put(13.0,35.0){\line{0}{-1}{10.0}}\put(30.0,30.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){$\scriptstyle\hskip-9.67383pt\nu_{y,z}$}}\put(30.0,36.0){\line{0}{-1}{1.0}}\put(30.0,24.0){\line{0}{1}{1.0}}\put(35.0,25.0){\line{-1}{0}{12.0}}\put(35.0,35.0){\line{-1}{0}{12.0}}\put(35.0,35.0){\line{0}{-1}{10.0}}\put(12.0,24.0){\oval(12.0,18.0)[b]}\put(12.0,15.0){\line{0}{-2}{3.0}}\put(30.0,24.0){\line{0}{-1}{12.0}}\put(12.0,0.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0)[b]{$\scriptstyle{\mathcal{F}}(xz)$}}\put(36.0,0.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0)[b]{$\scriptstyle{\mathcal{F}}(y)$}}\put(0.0,12.0){\line{1}{0}{0.0}}\end{picture}{\vskip 9.68745pt}\end{minipage}

for a bilax functor

(33)

Definition 5.1

A bilax natural transformation
\chi:{\mathcal{F}}\Rightarrow{\mathcal{F}}^{\prime}
between bilax functors is a pair
(\psi,\phi)
consisting of a lax
natural transformation
\psi
of lax functors and a colax natural transformation
\phi
of colax functors,
which agree on the 1-cell components,
i.e.

\psi(A)=\phi(A):=\chi(A)
for every
A\in{\mathcal{O}b}{\mathcal{K}}
, and whose 2-cell components are related through the relation:

(34)

for composable 1-cells:

.

(35)

which we will call the

above are over

) we will call

).

Example 5.2

Let
{\mathcal{K}}
and
{\mathcal{K}}^{\prime}
be 2-categories with Yang-Baxter operators
c
and
d
, respectively.
We fix two
d
-bimonads
b_{0}:A_{0}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A_{0}
and
b_{1}:A_{1}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A_{1}
in
{\mathcal{K}}^{\prime}
and define
bilax functors
{\mathcal{F}}_{0},{\mathcal{F}}_{1}:{\mathcal{K}}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}{\mathcal{K}}^{\prime}
by
{\mathcal{F}}_{0}:={\mathcal{F}}_{b_{0}}
and
{\mathcal{F}}_{1}:={\mathcal{F}}_{b_{1}}
with
\nu_{i}=d
for
i=1,2
, as in Example
4.11
.

A bilax natural transformation
(\chi,\psi,\phi)
between bilax functors
\chi:{\mathcal{F}}_{0}\Rightarrow{\mathcal{F}}_{1}

consists of a family of 2-cells

\psi_{A}:b_{1}\chi(A)\Rightarrow\chi(A)b_{0}\hskip 9.24994pt\text{and}\hskip 9.24994pt\phi_{A}:\chi(A)b_{0}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}b_{1}\chi(A),

indexed by
A\in{\mathcal{O}b}{\mathcal{K}}
, where
\psi_{A}
(respectively
\phi_{A}
) are distributive laws with respect to the monad (resp. comonad)
structures of
b_{0},b_{1}
(by (
12
) and its vertical dual), and
it holds:

and that the third equation in (
12
) is now trivial.
For every
A\in{\mathcal{O}b}{\mathcal{K}}
, the triple
(\chi(A),\psi_{A},\phi_{A})
is a 1-cell in the 2-category
{\rm Bimnd}({\mathcal{K}}^{\prime})
from
[
11
, Section 7]
,
which we mentioned at the beginning of Subsection
4.3
. The 0-cells of
{\rm Bimnd}({\mathcal{K}})
are bimonads in
{\mathcal{K}}
defined via a distributive
law 2-cell
\lambda
, 1-cells are triples
(F,\psi,\phi)
where
(F,\psi)
is a 1-cell in
{\rm Mnd}({\mathcal{K}})
and
(F,\phi)
is a 1-cell
in
{\rm Comnd}({\mathcal{K}})
with a compatibility condition between
\psi,\phi
and
\lambda
(as in (
36
) on the left),
and a 2-cell is a single 2-cell
\zeta
in
{\mathcal{K}}
which is simultaneously a 2-cell in
{\rm Mnd}({\mathcal{K}})
and in
{\rm Comnd}({\mathcal{K}})
.

Example 5.3

If
{\mathcal{K}}=Del({\mathcal{C}})
in the above Example is induced by a braided monoidal category
{\mathcal{C}}
,
the above bilax natural transformation
\chi:{\mathcal{F}}_{0}\Rightarrow{\mathcal{F}}_{1}:Del({\mathcal{C}})\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}{\mathcal{K}}^{\prime}
is precisely a single 1-cell in
{\rm Bimnd}(Del({\mathcal{C}}))
.

Example 5.4

By Lemma
4.8
actually
any
two bilax functors
({\mathcal{T}}_{0},\nu),({\mathcal{T}}_{1},\nu^{\prime}):1\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}{\mathcal{K}}
determine two bimonads in
{\mathcal{K}}
:

{\mathcal{T}}_{0}
yields a
\nu
-bimonad
b_{0}:={\mathcal{T}}(id_{*})
on
A={\mathcal{T}}(*)
and
{\mathcal{T}}_{1}
a
\nu^{\prime}
-bimonad
b_{1}:={\mathcal{T}}_{1}(id_{*})

on
A^{\prime}={\mathcal{T}}^{\prime}(*)
. Then analogously as in Example
5.3
, any bilax natural transformation
\chi:{\mathcal{T}}_{0}\Rightarrow{\mathcal{T}}_{1}:1\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}{\mathcal{K}}^{\prime}
is
precisely a single 1-cell in
{\rm Bimnd}({\mathcal{K}})
.

Example 5.5

Consider a bilax transformation
\chi:({\mathcal{T}},\nu)\Rightarrow({\mathcal{T}}^{\prime},\nu^{\prime}):1\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}{\mathcal{K}}
from the trivial 2-category to a 2-category
{\mathcal{K}}
.
Let
B:={\mathcal{T}}(id_{*})
be the
\nu
-bimonad on
{\mathcal{A}}={\mathcal{T}}(*)
and
B^{\prime}:={\mathcal{T}}^{\prime}(id_{*})
the
\nu^{\prime}
-bimonad
on
{\mathcal{T}}^{\prime}(*)
as in Lemma
4.8
. Let
m(B)
denote the monad part of
B
and
c(B)
the comonad part of
B
, and set
\chi(*)=X
. We find that

\psi:m(B^{\prime})X\Rightarrow Xm(B)
is a distributive law with respect to monads, and that
\phi:Xc(B)\Rightarrow c(B^{\prime})X
is a distributive law with respect to comonads.

It is a nice exercise to prove the following lemma that we will use to pursue with this example.

Lemma 5.6

B
be both a monad (to which we refer to as
m(B)

c(B)

m(B)

c(B)

(39)

The category of (left) Yetter-Drinfel‘d modules over
B
in
{\mathcal{C}}
and left
B
-linear and
B
-colinear morphisms we denote by

{}^{B}_{B}{\mathcal{Y}D}({\mathcal{C}})
.

Remark 5.7

Observe that the antipode,
i.e.
a Hopf algebra structure on a bialgebra in the context of Yetter-Drinfel’d modules, is
used in the following two instances. One is to construct the inverse for the braiding of the respective category. Another one is
to formulate an equivalent condition to (
39
). Thus, the category of Yetter-Drinfel’d modules
over a bialgebra is monoidal and even it has a pre-braiding (non-invertible), given by:

Example 5.8

Consider two braided monoidal categories
{\mathcal{C}}
and
{\mathcal{D}}
and two bialgebras
B_{0},B_{1}
in
{\mathcal{D}}
. These give rise to two bilax
(and bimonoidal) functors
F_{B_{0}},F_{B_{1}}:Del({\mathcal{C}})\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}Del({\mathcal{D}})
as in Example
4.11
.
The bilax natural transformation
\chi:{\mathcal{F}}_{0}\Rightarrow{\mathcal{F}}_{1}:{\mathcal{K}}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}{\mathcal{K}}^{\prime}
from Example
5.2
corresponds to a generalized notion of a
Yetter-Drinfel‘d module over
B_{1}
, which in view of the above we call
strong Yetter-Drinfel‘d modules
.

Proposition 5.9

Any Yetter-Drinfel’d module
M
over a bialgebra
B^{\prime}
in a braided monoidal category
({\mathcal{C}},\Phi)
comes from a bilax natural transformation
of Example
5.8
where
\psi
and
\phi
are given by

Proof.

The notation in these two diagrams is the usual one for braided monoidal categories, concretely

and

stand for the (co)multiplication of
B^{\prime}
.
That the given
\psi
and
\phi
are desired distributive laws (
i.e.
(co)lax natural transformations) it was proved at
the beginning of
[
10
, Section 5.1]
, though for
B=B^{\prime}
and trivial
j
.
The algebra (resp. coalgebra) morphism property of
j^{-1}
(resp.
j
) makes (
40
) the desired distributive laws
for nontrivial
j
. The first claim now follows from
[
11
, Proposition 7.5]
, whose conditions are fulfilled since
{\mathcal{C}}
is braided.
Set
\nu_{F,X}=(M\otimes j^{-1})\Phi_{F\hskip 1.70709pt^{\prime},X}
and
\nu_{X,F}=\Phi_{X,F\hskip 1.70709pt^{\prime}}(M\otimes j)
. The second claim follows from Corollary 7.6 of

loc. cit.
.
∎∎

Example 5.10

Let
(\chi,\psi,\phi)
be a bilax natural transformation between bilax functors with compatible Yang Baxter operators

\chi:{\mathcal{F}}\Rightarrow{\mathcal{F}}^{\prime}:Del({\mathcal{C}})\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}Del({\mathcal{D}})

where
{\mathcal{C}}
and
{\mathcal{D}}
are braided monoidal categories with braidings
\Phi_{\mathcal{C}}
and
\Phi_{\mathcal{D}}
, respectively.
Then
F:={\mathcal{F}}_{*,*}
and
G:={\mathcal{F}}^{\prime}_{*,*}
are bimonoidal functors
{\mathcal{C}}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}{\mathcal{D}}
as in Example
4.3
,
\chi(*)=M
is an object in
{\mathcal{D}}

and there are morphisms

\psi_{X}:G(X)\otimes M\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}M\otimes F(X)\hskip 9.24994pt\text{and}\hskip 9.24994pt\phi_{X}:M\otimes F(X)\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}G(X)\otimes M

natural in
X\in{\mathcal{C}}
, where
\psi
is a distributive law for the monoidal functor structures, and
\phi
is a distributive law
for the comonoidal functor structures, so that the left identity below holds, and consequently the one next to it:

For bialgebras
B
in
{\mathcal{C}}
by Proposition
4.9

F(B),G(B)
are bialgebras in
{\mathcal{D}}
, and if
\psi_{B},\phi_{B}
are of the form as in (
40
), we recover classical Yetter-Drinfel‘d modules in
{\mathcal{C}}
.

The bilax natural transformations,
i.e.
identities (
34
) and (
35
), offer the following
point of view. Given any monoidal category
{\mathcal{D}}
, when one considers the center category
{\mathcal{Z}}_{l}^{w}({\mathcal{D}})
, one is given a family of colax
transformations
\phi
. In particular, when
{\mathcal{D}}={}_{H}{\mathcal{C}}
the category of modules over a bialgebra or a Hopf algebra
H
in a braided
monoidal category
{\mathcal{C}}
, one is able to construct lax transformations
\psi
(as in (
40
)) so that the given

\phi
and this
\psi
obey (
35
) - since
\phi
is
H
-linear, being a morphism in
{}_{H}{\mathcal{C}}
. Similarly, considering
{\mathcal{Z}}_{r}^{w}({}^{H}{\mathcal{C}})

one is given
\psi
’s and one constructs
\phi
’s, so that they together obey (
35
). As in the proof of the above Proposition (that is,
as proved in
[
11
, Proposition 7.5]
), in this setting the bilax condition (
34
) follows.

5.2
Bilax modifications

We finally introduce:

Definition 5.11

Let
\chi,\chi^{\prime}:({\mathcal{F}},\nu)\Rightarrow({\mathcal{F}}^{\prime},\nu^{\prime}):({\mathcal{K}},c)\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}{\mathcal{K}}^{\prime}
be bilax natural transformations.
A
bilax modification

a:\chi\Rrightarrow\chi^{\prime}
is a collection of 2-cells
(a(A))_{A\in{\mathcal{O}b}({\mathcal{K}})}

(42)

Equivalently, a bilax modification is a modification both of lax and colax natural transformations:

a:\psi\Rrightarrow\psi^{\prime}
and
a:\phi\Rrightarrow\phi^{\prime}
,
where
(\psi,\phi)
constitute
\chi
and
(\psi^{\prime},\phi^{\prime})
constitute
\chi^{\prime}
.

Example 5.12

Pursuing Example
5.4
a bilax modification between bilax natural transformations of bilax functors
1\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}{\mathcal{K}}
is precisely a 2-cell in
{\rm Bimnd}({\mathcal{K}})
.

left
B^{\prime}
-comodules. This is a morphism of strong Yetter-Drinfel’d modules from Example
5.8
.

Now we may formulate:

Proposition 5.15

The category of Yetter-Drinfel‘d modules
{}^{B}_{B}{\mathcal{Y}D}({\mathcal{D}})
over a bialgebra
B
in a braided monoidal category
({\mathcal{D}},\Phi_{\mathcal{D}})
is a
full subcategory of the category
{\rm Bilax}({\mathcal{F}}_{B})
of bilax endo-transformations on a bilax functor
{\mathcal{F}}_{B}:(Del({\mathcal{C}}),\Phi_{\mathcal{C}})\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}(Del({\mathcal{D}}),\Phi_{\mathcal{D}})
with compatible Yang-Baxter operator as in Example
5.8
and bilax modifications.

Similarly one has:

Proposition 5.16

The category
{}^{B}_{B}{\mathcal{Y}D}({\mathcal{D}})
is isomorphic to the category
{\rm Bilax}({\mathcal{T}}_{B})
of bilax endo-transformations on a bilax functor

{\mathcal{T}}_{B}:1\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}(Del({\mathcal{D}}),\Phi_{\mathcal{D}})
with compatible Yang-Baxter operator as in Lemma
4.7
and bilax modifications.

5.3
2-category of bilax functors

We finish this section by concluding that bilax functors (with compatible Yang-Baxter operator)
{\mathcal{K}}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}{\mathcal{K}}^{\prime}
,
bilax natural transformations and bilax modifications form a 2-category
{\rm Bilax}({\mathcal{K}},{\mathcal{K}}^{\prime})
(respectively,
{\rm Bilax}_{c}({\mathcal{K}},{\mathcal{K}}^{\prime})
).
The composition of bilax transformations
(\chi,\psi,\phi):{\mathcal{F}}\Rightarrow{\mathcal{G}}
and
(\chi^{\prime},\psi^{\prime},\phi^{\prime}):{\mathcal{G}}\Rightarrow{\mathcal{H}}

is easily seen to be induced by the vertical compositions of the (co)lax transformations
\psi^{\prime}\cdot\psi,\phi^{\prime}\cdot\phi
, namely by:

Bilax modifications compose both horizontally and vertically, in the obvious and natural way.

We comment for the record that although the lax and colax natural transformations compose horizontally by:

(43)

the horizontal composition of lax and colax natural transformations does not induce a bilax
transformation. Namely, in order for this to work, the (co)lax structures should be identities.

We finally compare the 2-category
{\rm Bilax}({\mathcal{K}},{\mathcal{K}}^{\prime})
, more precisely its special case
{\rm Bilax}(1,{\mathcal{K}})
,
with two other existing 2-categories in the literature, namely
\operatorname{Dist}({\mathcal{K}})
and
{\rm Bimnd}({\mathcal{K}})
mentioned before.
From Lemma
4.8
, Example
5.4
and Example
5.12
we clearly have:

Proposition 5.17

There is a 2-category isomoprhism

{\rm Bilax}(1,{\mathcal{K}})\cong{\rm Bimnd}({\mathcal{K}}).

From Lemma
4.8
, Example
5.5
and Example
5.13
,
it can be appreciated that on the level of 1- and 2-cells there is a faithful assignment
{\rm Bimnd}({\mathcal{K}})\hookrightarrow\operatorname{Dist}({\mathcal{K}}).

Since the 0-cells of
\operatorname{Dist}({\mathcal{K}})
are given by tupples
({\mathcal{A}},T,D,\lambda)
, where
T
is a monad
and
D
a comonad on a 0-cell
{\mathcal{A}}
in
{\mathcal{K}}
, and
\lambda:TD\Rightarrow DT
is a distributive law with respect to monad and comonad as in
(
37
), we clearly have:

Proposition 5.18

There is a faithful 2-functor

{\rm Bimnd}({\mathcal{K}})\hookrightarrow\operatorname{Dist}({\mathcal{K}}),

which is defined on 0-cells by
({\mathcal{A}},B,\nu)\mapsto({\mathcal{A}},m(B),c(B),\lambda)
, with
\lambda
being

\lambda(\nu):=\begin{minipage}[48.0pt]\begin{picture}\put(0.0,48.0){\line{1}{0}{0.0}}\put(12.0,52.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0)[b]{$\scriptstyle B$}}\put(30.0,52.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0)[b]{$\scriptstyle B$}}\put(12.0,36.0){\oval(12.0,18.0)[t]}\put(12.0,48.0){\line{0}{-1}{3.0}}\put(30.0,48.0){\line{0}{-1}{12.0}}\put(6.0,36.0){\line{0}{-1}{12.0}}\put(18.0,36.0){\line{0}{-1}{1.0}}\put(18.0,24.0){\line{0}{1}{1.0}}\put(13.0,25.0){\line{1}{0}{12.0}}\put(13.0,35.0){\line{1}{0}{12.0}}\put(13.0,35.0){\line{0}{-1}{10.0}}\put(30.0,30.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){$\scriptstyle\hskip-9.67383pt\nu$}}\put(30.0,36.0){\line{0}{-1}{1.0}}\put(30.0,24.0){\line{0}{1}{1.0}}\put(35.0,25.0){\line{-1}{0}{12.0}}\put(35.0,35.0){\line{-1}{0}{12.0}}\put(35.0,35.0){\line{0}{-1}{10.0}}\put(12.0,24.0){\oval(12.0,18.0)[b]}\put(12.0,15.0){\line{0}{-2}{3.0}}\put(30.0,24.0){\line{0}{-1}{12.0}}\put(12.0,0.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0)[b]{$\scriptstyle B$}}\put(30.0,0.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0)[b]{$\scriptstyle B.$}}\put(0.0,12.0){\line{1}{0}{0.0}}\end{picture}{\vskip 9.68745pt}\end{minipage}

Observe that Proposition
5.16
is a consequence of the above 2-category isomorphism
{\rm Bilax}(1,{\mathcal{K}})\cong{\rm Bimnd}({\mathcal{K}})
.
